A response with no usable dispatch record (an id the server never issued, a failed record write, a record reaped after its 24 hour window, or a lookup that failed twice) is stored `~unverified`, mints no row and moves no reason of `yuzu_server_registry_audit_dropped_total`; the counters that move are `yuzu_server_response_provenance_total{outcome}` (`unrecorded` or `lookup_degraded`) and, for a failed record write, `yuzu_exec_correlation_write_degrade_total`. **Fields:** `principal=agent:<agent_id>` (the agent id the session is bound to: certificate-bound when the agent registered with a client certificate under mTLS enforcement, otherwise bound by session and peer address), `principal_role=agent`, `target_type=command`, `target_id=<command_id>`. `detail` is `agent=<agent_id>`, a space, `cut=0` or `cut=1` (1 when the line was cut at the byte bound; see Bounds) and a space, then the line: the server's tokens come first, so a reader that takes the first `agent=` or the first `cut=` match never finds one from inside the endpoint's text. `result=success` only when the whole line's first `\|result=` token is `ok` (read from the whole line, before it is cut); any other value, or none, is `result=failure` -- never derived from `dry_run`. **Registry values can hold secrets (an autologon password, a product key): treat these rows as sensitive. Owner risk acceptance: old and new values are recorded as raw hex and can contain secrets; the product owner accepted this risk on 2026-10-20 and masking is a planned later change. A row is evidence of a change (except a `dry_run=1` row, which records that a vet ran), not a restore point. The same holds for the `apply` and per-user rows. The write parameters are stored unredacted in `executions.parameter_values`, which no server retention pass prunes: `value` and `data_hex` of `set_value` and `set_user_value`, and the whole `apply` `plan`. The responses carry the same old values, and an `apply` reply's undo manifest holds the previous values (up to 4,096 bytes each, 512 KiB per reply) for `Response:Read` holders for 90 days by default.** A recursive `delete_key` row looks like a leaf delete apart from its counts: `recursive` is only in `executions.parameter_values`, joined on the `command_id`. The rows are readable by every role holding `AuditLog:Read` (Viewer, Operator, PlatformEngineer, ITServiceOwner and Administrator; 365-day audit retention by default), by MCP `query_audit_log`, by the stored response through `Response:Read` (the same five roles; 90-day response store by default), and, through the legacy `/events` stream, by any authenticated session that is not service-scoped, regardless of RBAC. The `command_id` joins to the dispatching operator's row where one exists: the `command.dispatch` / `instruction.execute` row for `/api/command` and the dashboard (same `target_id`); an MCP `execute_instruction` row names the tool as its target with the `command_id` in its detail; `POST /api/instructions/{id}/execute` uses the definition id as target; a bundle step joins by `correlation_id`; a scheduled run is paired by its `command.outbox_delivered` row (see `docs/observability-conventions.md`), whose `target_id` is the `command_id`. A workflow step (`workflow.execute`), a result-set producer (`result_set.create`) and a compliance remediation (`policy.remediate`, always operator-initiated) record only an `execution_id`, so this row's `target_id` is the only handle on the `command_id`. **Bounds:** at most 300 lines of 4,096 bytes per output frame are taken; lines past the 300th are not recorded; a longer line is cut on a UTF-8 character boundary (other bytes are cut at the bound) and its row says `cut=1`; ASCII control bytes in a line are replaced with `_`. **Budget:** each agent id may write 600 rows per fixed 60-second window (a burst straddling a window boundary can admit up to twice that) on each server process (an HA replica keeps its own, and a restart resets it), because a bound per frame is not a bound on frames. Rows over it are refused, not queued. An `apply` of 256 items is up to 256 rows (inside the 300-line bound even when one frame carries them all), so three such plans inside one window on one agent exceed the budget and the later rows are suppressed. The first 262,144 agents to write in a window each have an allowance; later agents share 65,536 allowances by a hash of the id, so a fleet-wide change answered at about one row per agent still loses nothing (at 1.2 million agents an agent that writes twenty rows or more in one window can start to lose some), while an agent that loops rows there exhausts the allowance it shares. Refused rows are counted, and one log line per window reports their number (count only, naming no agent), logged when the next window opens. **Signals:** the rows of a frame are written before its execution advances, and the writes are synchronous (one commit per row): a failing audit write never stops an execution, but the frame waits for it. A failed store write ends that frame's rows, is logged, moves `yuzu_server_audit_emit_failed_total` (alert `YuzuAuditPersistFailures`) and, on the direct session, adds the gRPC `x-yuzu-audit-failed` trailer once per stream, delivered when the stream closes (nothing in the agent reads it; the gateway path has no trailer, so the one log line the hook writes for the failed frame is its signal). At shutdown `ServerImpl::stop()` closes the hook and waits up to 15 seconds for a write in progress before it releases the audit store and the database pool (a write still stuck then ends the process, as the neighbouring shutdown waits do): the rows of a frame that are not yet attempted when the hook closes are counted as `persist_failed` and logged once at warning level (the counter is this process's own and its `/metrics` listener is already stopped by then, so that log line is the evidence; on the direct session the call also gets the audit-failed trailer), and a frame that arrives afterwards records and counts nothing. `yuzu_server_registry_audit_dropped_total{reason}` counts the evidence the server sees go unrecorded while its audit store is up: `suppressed` (rows refused by the budget), `persist_failed` (rows lost to a failed write, or not attempted because shutdown closed the hook mid-frame), `unmintable_id` (registry mutating frames whose `command_id` names the plugin but is not the minted shape: no rows, logged once per frame; an empty id names no plugin) and `truncated` (frames that hit a bound, logged once per frame). To find the agent behind a climbing `suppressed`, count the persisted `registry.write` rows per `principal`. **Rows can be lost, uncounted,** when a stream drops after a mutation (the frames not yet delivered), when a command finishes more than 300 seconds after dispatch for a gateway-connected agent (the gateway's command window: its later frames are dropped), for frames that arrive once shutdown has closed the hook (just before the server releases its audit store), when a registry command has no dispatch record (the frame is labelled `~unverified`; see Binding), when an allocation failure stops the server working out a frame's rows (only an error log line says so), when the agent process dies between a write and its reply (the stored response and the `command.dispatch` row keep the attempt attributable; the registry may hold the write), and when the agent cannot even write its exception line. An exception inside a mutating action on the agent is not a lost row: the plugin writes a fixed `result=failed` `reason=exception` line before it answers `error|exception`, which the server records like any other. Such a row does not say whether the write landed. A creating write tries to roll back the keys it made while the exception comes before its verified write ends, and that rollback is BEST EFFORT: a key holding a value or a subkey when the rollback checks it is kept and, on a returning exit, counted in `created=`; a write that lands between the check and the delete, or over our own same-named value, can still be lost; and after a double fault (a rollback step that fails or throws) the value and the keys may stay with `created=-` and nothing logged. A write that had already landed on an existing key keeps its value, and an exception after the verified write of a creating write (while the reply is being built) leaves the verified value and the keys, recorded as `result=failed` `reason=exception` with `created=-`. The exception row has `hive=-|key=-|created=-`, so take the hive, key and value name from the dispatching request or from `executions.parameter_values` joined on the `command_id`, and follow the partial-create procedure in the plugin README ("A partial or failed create"), deleting only keys whose creation by this call can be shown. **Producer contract** (the `registry` plugin emits the lines, 2.1.0 for the six actions; its README publishes the field grammar and pins it against this parser; one trailing CR is tolerated): one record on each LF-terminated line; no `\|`, LF or CR inside a field, replaced or percent-encoded (a key or value name can contain them, and a backslash-escaped `\|` still reads as a `\|` here, so that text could pick the row's `result` or forge a second row); `result=` before any caller-sized field; one record per operation (a `delete_key` is one line: it removes one key, or with `recursive` one tree, and the counts ride in fields; an `apply` is one line per plan item it attempts). |
| `instruction.approve` | Instruction | An instruction pending approval is approved |
| `instruction.deny` | Instruction | An instruction pending approval is denied |
| `instruction_set.create` / `instruction_set.delete` | InstructionSet | Written twice per call: `result=attempted` BEFORE the change (if it cannot be stored the request is refused with `503` and `Sec-Audit-Failed: true`, and nothing is created or deleted), then the outcome (`success`, `denied`, `error`). See the REST API reference for the detail values. |
| `csrf.denied` (central gate) | Endpoint | Any cookie-session request other than `GET`/`HEAD`/`OPTIONS` that carried no `Origin`/`Referer`, or one naming another site (and not a `--csrf-trusted-origin` entry), and any guarded cookie-session request (one that dispatches work, persists state or forces a refresh of server state, whatever its method) whose `Sec-Fetch-Site` header is present and is not exactly one `same-origin` or `none` value (a present header decides alone; only when it is absent is the `Origin`/`Referer` test applied), refused `403` by the server-wide same-site check, after authentication and before step-up evaluation, challenge emission and route-handler effects, and before the body is read. `target_id` is the sanitised request method and path, with the request path truncated at 200 bytes (for example `POST /path`, or `GET /path` for a guarded read); `result=denied`; `detail` is the fixed text `cross-site or header-less browser write refused; reason=<token>`, where `<token>` is one of the closed set `no_origin_no_referer`, `origin_mismatch`, `referer_mismatch` (no header bytes are ever recorded; a guarded read uses the same fixed text, so it still reads "browser write refused", with `origin_mismatch` for any refused `Sec-Fetch-Site` value; a refusal caused by a repeated `Sec-Fetch-Site` header is likewise recorded with reason `origin_mismatch`, since the reason set is closed); the principal is the signed-in operator. One row per refused request. A request that presents a valid session cookie is checked as a cookie-session request even if it also carries a token; requests authenticated only by a token are not gated. If the row cannot be written the response still refuses and carries `Sec-Audit-Failed: true`. If the check itself fails (counter `reason` `gate_error`) the request is refused and counted but this path writes no row of its own, which is why the shipped `YuzuSameSiteGateInternalError` alert exists. A deployment with no audit store configured (audit off) writes no row at all and the response carries no `Sec-Audit-Failed` header, since nothing failed to persist: the counter `yuzu_server_csrf_denied_total` is then the only record. For a cookie-session caller this row replaces the per-route `csrf.denied` rows below, so `target_id` values keyed on those no longer appear for cookie-session callers; since this release they appear only for a token caller of a route whose own copy is not token-exempt. Rows written by those copies may use `result=error` with sanitised header bytes, while this row always uses `result=denied` with fixed text. |
| `csrf.denied` (user management) | Endpoint | A browser (session-cookie) `POST /api/settings/users`, `DELETE /api/settings/users/{username}` or `POST /api/settings/users/{username}/role` carried no `Origin`/`Referer`, or one naming another site, and was refused with `403` before anything was read or changed. `target_id` is the route (`/api/settings/users`, `/api/settings/users/{username}`, `/api/settings/users/{username}/role`). API-token callers are exempt. For a cookie-session caller the `csrf.denied` (central gate) row is written instead. |
| `csrf.denied` (instruction family) | Endpoint | A browser (session-cookie) write to an instruction, instruction-set, response-template or `/api/instructions/{id}/execute` route that carried no `Origin`/`Referer`, or one naming another site, was refused with `403`. Token-authenticated callers are not gated. For a cookie-session caller the `csrf.denied` (central gate) row is written instead. |
| `csrf.denied` (dashboard dispatch) | Endpoint | A browser (session-cookie) `POST /api/dashboard/execute` or `POST /api/dashboard/tar-execute` that carried no `Origin`/`Referer`, or one naming another site, was refused with `403` before anything was parsed, audited as attempted or dispatched. `target_id` is `dashboard`. Token-authenticated callers are not gated. For a cookie-session caller the `csrf.denied` (central gate) row is written instead. |
| `csrf.denied` (access reviews) | Endpoint | A browser (session-cookie) `POST /api/v1/access-reviews`, `POST /api/v1/access-reviews/{id}/attestations` or `POST /api/v1/access-reviews/{id}/close` carried no `Origin`/`Referer`, or one naming another site, and was refused with `403` before anything was read or changed. `target_id` is the route as written. API-token callers are exempt. A body that is not `application/json` on the first two is refused with `415` and an `access_review.campaign_opened` or `access_review.attested` row, `result=denied`, `detail=content_type_not_json`. For a cookie-session caller the `csrf.denied` (central gate) row is written instead. |
| `csrf.denied` (privileged RBAC and Guardian writes) | Endpoint | A browser (session-cookie) call to one of the privileged RBAC or Guardian write routes (each is marked "Cross-site writes" in the REST API reference) carried no `Origin`/`Referer`, or one naming another site (and not a `--csrf-trusted-origin` entry), and was refused with `403`. The central check (first `csrf.denied` row of this group) decides this first; each of these routes keeps its own copy after its authorization gates and any MFA step-up and before the body was read, as defence in depth. `target_id` is a short per-route label. The refusal is recorded under `csrf.denied`, not under the route's own verb. API, MCP and engine token callers are not gated. |
| `instruction.import` | InstructionDefinition | An InstructionDefinition JSON envelope was submitted via `POST /api/instructions/import`. On `result=success`: `target_id` is the new definition's id; `detail` is empty. On `result=denied`: `target_id` is empty (no id assigned on rejection); `detail` carries the store-returned error string, which begins with a stable SIEM-keyable token - known tokens are `duplicate_id` (409 conflict), `signature verification failed for instruction — content may have been tampered with` (#1073 / W7.4), `instruction-import is unsigned and signature enforcement is enabled` (#1073), `instruction-import has incomplete signing metadata` (#1073), `instruction-import has signing field of wrong JSON type` (#1073 R1), `signature length invalid` / `publicKey length invalid` (#1073 R1 DoS amplification guard), `instruction-import has signature + publicKey but no yaml_source` (#1073), `definition id may not use the reserved 'mcp.' prefix` (#2442 - the `mcp.` definition-id namespace belongs to MCP approval tickets; note this one is reached AFTER signature verification, so a signed pack is verified first and then refused on the id), `definition id may not use the reserved 'guardian.' prefix` (#6062 - the `guardian.` namespace belongs to Guardian rule-deploy approval tickets; same ordering as the `mcp.` token), and the write-time parameter-schema denials `parameter_schema is larger than the 262144-byte limit`, `parameter_schema is not a valid parameter schema: <up to 3 problems>[; and N more]` and `parameter_schema could not be checked` (the problems name only a property and a fixed reason, never a schema value). Permission gate: `InstructionDefinition:Write`. SOC 2 CC6.7: every import attempt is logged regardless of outcome; if the audit-store write itself fails, the response carries the `Sec-Audit-Failed: true` header AND an `audit_emitted=false` field in the JSON body (PR #883 evidence-chain pattern). |
| `enrollment.approve` | Agent | A pending agent enrollment is approved from the dashboard (`POST /api/settings/pending-agents/{id}/approve`). `target_id` is the agent id; `detail` carries `by=<operator>` (and `agent_not_found` with `result=denied` when no such row exists; `refused_wrong_status` with `result=denied` and HTTP 409 when the row is no longer `pending`, because it was already approved or denied, so an approve never reverses a denial; `invalid_agent_id` with `result=denied` and HTTP 400 when the id is `.` or `..`, which no browser link can address). A store outage audits `result=failure`. Set-and-proceed, like every dashboard settings mutation. |
| `enrollment.deny` | Agent | A pending agent enrollment is denied (`POST /api/settings/pending-agents/{id}/deny`). Same shape as `enrollment.approve`, including `refused_wrong_status` (a deny never flips an enrolled agent). A denied agent is refused at Register before any token is consumed (#1067), and a token presented by a denied agent has its use rolled back (#1135). |
| `enrollment.bulk_approve` / `enrollment.bulk_deny` | Agent | `POST /api/settings/pending-agents/bulk-approve` / `bulk-deny`. One atomic statement moves only the rows that are `pending` at that instant, so a concurrent single deny/approve is never overwritten. `target_id` empty; `detail` is `by=<operator> count=<N> ids=<first 20 agent ids>` where `count` is the number of rows THIS call moved. |
| `enrollment.remove` | Agent | A pending-agent row is hard-deleted (`DELETE /api/settings/pending-agents/{id}`) — **only when the row is still `pending`** (WS-6 6.2 hardening; removal never reverses an admin decision). `result=success`, `detail=by=<operator>` when a pending row is removed; `result=denied`, `detail=invalid_agent_id by=<operator>` (HTTP 400) for an id of `.` or `..`. `result=denied`, `detail=refused_wrong_status by=<operator>` (`409`) when the row is `approved` or `denied` — the row is left untouched and the earlier decision stands. `result=denied`, `detail=agent_not_found by=<operator>` when no such row exists. `result=failure`, `detail=store_error by=<operator>` on a store outage. |
| `enrollment.token_create` | EnrollmentToken | An enrollment token is generated (`POST /api/settings/enrollment-tokens`). `target_id` is the 8-hex token id — **never the raw token**; `detail` carries `by=`, `max_uses=`, `ttl_s=` and the label (space, `=`, `,` and control bytes replaced by `_`). A malformed `max_uses` or `ttl_hours`, or a negative `ttl_hours`, emits `result=denied` with an empty `target_id` and `detail=invalid_numeric_parameter` alongside the `400`. |
| `enrollment.bulk_token_create` | EnrollmentToken | `POST /api/settings/enrollment-tokens/batch`, and the server CLI `--generate-tokens` (principal `cli:<OS account>`, role `cli`). `target_id` empty; `detail` carries `by=`, `max_uses=`, `ttl_s=`, the label prefix (space, `=`, `,` and control bytes replaced by `_`) and `count=<N> ids=<first 20 token ids>`. On the REST route, a mid-batch store failure audits `result=failure` with `minted_before_failure=<N>`; a malformed `count`, `max_uses` or `ttl_hours`, or a negative `ttl_hours`, emits `result=denied` with `detail=invalid_numeric_parameter`; a `count` outside 1 to 10000 emits `result=denied` with `detail=count_out_of_range`, each alongside a `400`; and a body that is not one JSON object emits `result=denied` with `detail=invalid_body`. The CLI writes one `result=success` row after the whole batch is minted and no row otherwise: a mid-batch store failure or a refused option exits non-zero and logs how many tokens were minted before the failure, and those tokens remain valid. |
| `enrollment.token_revoke` | EnrollmentToken | A token is revoked (`DELETE /api/settings/enrollment-tokens/{id}`). `result=denied` with `token_not_found` for an unknown id. |
| `enrollment.import` | Enrollment | The one-time boot import of the legacy `enrollment-tokens.cfg` / `pending-agents.cfg` into PostgreSQL (WS-6 6.2). Principal `system`. `target_id` is the file kind (`tokens` / `pending`); `detail` carries `source=<path> sha256=<fingerprint> imported= skipped_existing= skipped_garbled= recovered_colon= id_disambiguated= renamed=`. `result=success` for a first import; `result=warning` when a file that differs from the one already imported (a restored old backup) was **refused**; `result=failure` for an import error. The steady-state outcomes (no file / already imported) write no row. |
| `enrollment.token_consumed` | EnrollmentToken | An agent auto-enrolled by presenting an enrollment token (gRPC Register — direct or gateway-proxied). `result=success` when the token is accepted; `result=failure` when rejected (invalid/expired/already-consumed). `target_id` is the token id (success) or the token-hash prefix (failure); `detail` carries `variant=<not_found\|revoked\|expired\|already_consumed>` on a failure row (`invalid_input` and `internal_error` are not produced on this path) and `variant=success use_count=<n>/<max or unlimited>` on success, and, on the gateway path, `source=gateway_proxy gateway_ip=<ip> [origin_observed=false]`. **Gateway-path `source_ip` (#1064):** the server records the agent's origin IP as `source_ip` when the gateway populates `RegisterRequest.gateway_observed_peer`. The gateway overwrites that field on every registration (whatever the agent sent is discarded) with the address it decided for the call, a bare IP, or with an empty value meaning "not observed", in which case `source_ip` is the gateway node's own IP and `detail` carries `origin_observed=false`. By listener configuration: a listener in `transport` mode (the default) reports the TCP peer; a listener configured as proxied (`origin` mode `proxy_protocol` or `forwarded_for`) reports the address the trusted proxy vouched for, reports empty when that proxy gave no usable address (`origin_observed=false`; the gateway counts `yuzu_gw_grpc_origin_ignored_total{reason="no_claim"|"bad_claim"}`), and reports the TCP peer for a connection that did not come from one of the configured proxies. **A listener left in `transport` mode behind a load balancer or router that was never configured records that proxy's address as `source_ip` with no `origin_observed=false`, so many agents sharing one `source_ip` is the sign.** The audit row records the address (or `origin_observed=false` when none was observed), not how the gateway obtained it: it does not distinguish the TCP peer, a proxy-vouched address and a `transport`-mode listener behind an unconfigured proxy; a separate, later change will add that. A gateway older than this behaviour forwards whatever the agent sent, and the server cannot tell the two apart: upgrade every gateway before relying on `source_ip` (see [Client addresses behind a proxy](gateway.md#client-addresses-behind-a-proxy)). On audit-store write failure the gRPC response carries `x-yuzu-audit-failed: true` trailing metadata and the analytics severity escalates to error (#1063 / #1065). |
| `settings.update` | Setting | A server setting is changed |
| `rbac.role.assigned` | User | A fleet-wide RBAC role was assigned to a human user via `POST /api/v1/rbac/roles/{name}/assignments` or the MCP `assign_rbac_role` twin (A2 — global human role assignment, see "Fleet-Wide Role Assignment" in `docs/user-manual/rbac.md`). `target_id` is the username, neutralized before it is embedded (a control-byte/CRLF/ANSI-escape payload cannot forge an adjacent audit field or inject terminal escapes into the trail meant to hold its author accountable — same neutralization the `principal_type` copy inside `detail` gets on a rejection) — except on the structural pre-gate denials below (service-scoped, engine-session, mcp-tier), which use an EMPTY `target_id`, since those deny before the route ever reaches the caller-supplied `principal_id`. `result=success` with `detail=<role_name>; target_provisioned=<true\|false\|unknown>` (whether the target already had an `auth.users` row at assignment time — `unknown` means the AuthDB read itself degraded and could not confirm either way, never conflated with a genuine `false`; pre-provisioning a role ahead of first login is allowed). `result=denied` when the caller is not a durable Administrator (re-read fresh from the store — a dedicated `is_rbac_administrator` gate, NOT an ordinary permission check), the gate itself could not confirm authority because the RBAC/`AuthDB` store is degraded (`kUnavailable`, `503`/`kInternalError`, `detail="RBAC admin gate could not confirm administrator authority — store degraded"` — also logged via `spdlog::warn`, so this outcome is never silently invisible to an operator), does not hold authority under the regime that is durably true right now (A1: a second, fresh re-check closing a cross-replica cache-staleness gap in that same gate — see "Enabling RBAC" in `docs/user-manual/rbac.md`; ITS OWN degraded-store outcome is a SEPARATE `detail="RBAC regime-authority check could not confirm authority — store degraded"`, also warned+audited so the two checks are individually diagnosable), is a service-scoped/engine session — **REST/MCP asymmetry**: on MCP specifically there is no separate engine-session pre-gate call (that helper is REST-only); an engine session instead falls through to `is_rbac_administrator(kMcp)`'s own internal check, auditing `target_id=<username>` (not empty) with the SAME `detail="caller is not a durable RBAC administrator"` an ordinary non-admin denial gets — indistinguishable from an ordinary denial in the audit log on that transport, (REST only) carries a non-empty `mcp_tier` — `detail="MCP token blocked from admin route (mcp_tier='<tier>')"` via the shared `deny_mcp_token_session` helper, denied BEFORE `is_rbac_administrator` even runs, since REST has no tier/approval concept to legitimize a tiered bearer token the way MCP's own dispatch guard does — `principal_type` is not `"user"`, `principal_id` is malformed, the role is not one of the 7 fleet-wide-assignable roles — **`principal_id` missing or wrong-typed is the one exception**: it 400s before `target_id` is computed and is deliberately left unaudited on this route (matching the pre-existing unaudited malformed-JSON-body branch), not a CWE-117 necessity, just this route's established convention (`ITServiceOwner` and an unknown/custom role share the SAME uniform client-facing message, no role-catalog oracle — the specific reason is in `detail` server-side only), or a genuine store/query fault occurred while writing the grant (`503`/`kInternalError`, distinguished from a client-validation rejection via an allow-list of known validation-error shapes — `rbac_store.hpp`'s `rbac_assign_error_is_client_fault`). |
| `rbac.role.unassigned` | User | A fleet-wide RBAC role was removed from a human user via `DELETE /api/v1/rbac/roles/{name}/assignments/{principal_id}` or the MCP `unassign_rbac_role` twin. `target_id` is the username (REST: URL-path-captured, already charset-safe by construction; MCP: neutralized before embedding, same as the assign twin) — except on the structural pre-gate denials below (service-scoped, engine-session, mcp-tier), which use an EMPTY `target_id`. `result=success` with `detail=<role_name>; removed=<true\|false>` — the unassign is **idempotent** (matching the engine-principal sibling below): removing a role the principal never held still returns `success`/`removed=false`, so an auditor can tell an actual revoke apart from a no-op from the log alone. On MCP, `role_name` inside `detail` is neutralized too (this tool's `role` arg is deliberately NOT charset-restricted, so it can clean up an out-of-band grant no other surface could have created). `result=denied` when the caller is not a durable Administrator, the gate itself could not confirm authority because the RBAC/`AuthDB` store is degraded (`kUnavailable`, `503`/`kInternalError`, `detail="RBAC admin gate could not confirm administrator authority — store degraded"` — also logged via `spdlog::warn`), does not hold authority under the regime that is durably true right now (A1: same fresh re-check as the assign twin above, including ITS OWN separate `detail="RBAC regime-authority check could not confirm authority — store degraded"` on a degraded read), is removing their own `Administrator` assignment (self-lockout guard, `detail=self_admin_unassign_blocked`), is a service-scoped/engine session — same REST/MCP asymmetry as the assign twin above (MCP has no separate engine-session pre-gate; it falls through to `is_rbac_administrator(kMcp)`'s own internal check, auditing `target_id=<username>` with the ordinary `detail="caller is not a durable RBAC administrator"`), (REST only) carries a non-empty `mcp_tier` — `detail="MCP token blocked from admin route (mcp_tier='<tier>')"` via the shared `deny_mcp_token_session` helper, denied BEFORE `is_rbac_administrator` even runs — or the store-level last-Administrator guard refuses to leave the fleet with zero **authenticatable** administrators (REST `409`; the guard joins against `auth.users` and requires `is_active`, so a grant naming a nonexistent, deactivated, soft-deleted, or group-held (not creatable via this surface) principal is never counted as a survivor — matching the `is_rbac_administrator` gate on the `principal_type` axis only (with RBAC off the gate reads `auth.users.role` instead of grants), closed against a concurrent reactivation of an existing account, with two narrower cases still outside that guard — see "Fleet-Wide Role Assignment" in `docs/user-manual/rbac.md`), or a genuine store/query fault occurred while removing the grant (`503`/`kInternalError`). |
| `rbac.enforcement_changed` | Setting | RBAC enforcement was enabled or disabled fleet-wide via `PUT /api/v1/rbac/enforcement` or the MCP `set_rbac_enforcement` twin (A1 — enforcement enable/disable toggle, see "Enabling RBAC" in `docs/user-manual/rbac.md`). `target_id` is the fixed literal `rbac_enabled` (the durable key) on every gate this route reaches on its own — but NOT on every denial path: the shared `deny_fleet_wide_service_scoped`, `deny_engine_session` (REST only — see the asymmetry note below), and `deny_mcp_token_session` structural pre-gate denials (below) use an EMPTY `target_id` on REST, since those chokepoints deny before this route's own handler ever runs. `result=success` with `detail=enabled=<true\|false>; previous=<true\|false>; changed=<true\|false>; post_transition_administrators=<N>` — `changed=false` marks the idempotent no-op path (requesting the already-current state; no write, no guard evaluation). `result=denied` with one of these detail shapes: `caller is not a durable RBAC administrator` (the same dedicated `is_rbac_administrator` gate as the two rows above, NOT an ordinary permission check); `RBAC admin gate could not confirm administrator authority — store degraded` (that SAME gate's `kUnavailable` outcome, also logged via `spdlog::warn`, `503`/`kInternalError` — never silently invisible to an operator); `service-scoped token blocked from RBAC enforcement toggle` — via the shared `deny_fleet_wide_service_scoped` helper, which appends `; cid=<uuid>` in practice, so this is never byte-exact as its own literal alone; the `deny_engine_session` structural denial (REST only — **REST/MCP asymmetry**, same shape as the mcp-tier one below: MCP has no separate engine-session pre-gate call; an engine session there instead falls through to `is_rbac_administrator(kMcp)`'s own internal check, auditing `target_id="rbac_enabled"` — not empty — with the ordinary `detail="caller is not a durable RBAC administrator"` above, not a distinct "engine session" literal); `MCP token blocked from admin route (mcp_tier='<tier>')` (REST only — the shared `deny_mcp_token_session` helper, same as the two rows above, denied BEFORE `is_rbac_administrator` even runs; REST has no tier/approval concept to legitimize a tiered bearer token the way MCP's own dispatch guard does); `empty mcp_tier, denied outright (auth_source=<...>)` (MCP only, #4309 parity with `assign_rbac_role`/`unassign_rbac_role` — a DIFFERENT check on a DIFFERENT transport from the REST-only one immediately above, despite the similar name); `guard: <refusal message>` — the store-level guard itself (`RbacStore::set_rbac_enforcement`) refusing on either of TWO checks, both prefixed identically so the audit row alone does not distinguish which one fired (the fixed, server-authored `<refusal message>` text does — never caller-supplied): a DESTINATION-regime survival conflict (the caller would not remain a durable administrator after the switch — enable: no fleet-wide `Administrator` grant; disable: local account role is not `admin`), or a SOURCE-regime authority failure (the caller does not hold authority under the regime that is durably true right now — enable: not a local `admin`; disable: no fleet-wide `Administrator` grant — this closes a cross-replica cache-staleness gap in the outer admin gate itself); or, a seventh case: `kStoreFailure`'s `detail` is typically the RAW underlying store-error text (a pool/txn/query failure), with one fixed fallback literal when that text is empty — either way it can carry implementation detail a SIEM rule matching on the other fixed literals above will not catch. Fails closed on a dropped success row on both transports (REST `503`; MCP `audit_persisted:false`), same #2466/#2406 posture as the two rows above. **Transport asymmetry:** on MCP specifically, the service-scoped-token denial above fires under a COMPLETELY DIFFERENT action/target — `mcp.set_rbac_enforcement`/`mcp_tool`/`set_rbac_enforcement`, via the generic C8 chokepoint (`#2298`) — never `rbac.enforcement_changed` at all for this one case; a SIEM query filtering on `action="rbac.enforcement_changed"` alone will silently miss an MCP-side service-scoped denial. |
| `rbac.assignments.list` | AccessReview | A caller pulled the complete fleet-wide RBAC grant table via `GET /api/v1/rbac/roles/assignments`, the dashboard's Role assignments panel (`GET /fragments/access/roles/assignments`, same gate and same row; a denied engine session writes `result=denied`, `detail=engine_session_denied`) or the MCP `list_rbac_role_assignments` twin — every `(principal_type, principal_id, role_name)` row on record, across all three principal types, in one bulk read (reuses `RbacStore::list_all_principal_roles_checked()`, the same method the SOC 2 CC6.2 access-review export uses). `target_id` is empty (a fleet-wide read, not a single-target one); `result=success` with `detail=count=<N>`. Gated on `AccessReview:Read`, the same dedicated securable the access-review family below uses — not audited on a genuine store-query failure on REST (fire-and-forget, matching the `access_review.*` family's own list-route template; the MCP twin sets `audit_persisted:false` in its response body instead of a separate audited-failure row — both asymmetries are pre-existing, family-wide, tracked in #5063, not specific to this action). **Note:** the `access_review.*` action family this route reuses the read path from does write audit rows of its own, from the REST routes and their MCP twins (`access_review.exported`, `access_review.list`, `access_review.campaign_opened`, `access_review.get`, `access_review.attested`, `access_review.flagged` for a `flagged_revoke` decision, and `access_review.closed`); they are not yet catalogued as separate rows in this table (a pre-existing gap, not introduced here). |
| `management_group.create` | ManagementGroup | A management group is created |
| `management_group.delete` | ManagementGroup | A management group is deleted |
| `management_group.add_member` | ManagementGroup | An agent is added to a management group |
| `management_group.remove_member` | ManagementGroup | An agent is removed from a management group |
| `group.create_from_results` | ManagementGroup | A new management group is materialised from a filtered results view via `POST /api/dashboard/group-from-results` (#1712). `target_id` is the new group's id once created; empty on the pre-creation `denied` CSRF path below, since no group exists yet. `result=success`: the group and all requested members were created; `detail` empty. `result=denied`: the CSRF same-site gate rejected the request (`detail=csrf_cross_origin`) before any read or write — no group is created, so `target_id` is empty. `result=failure`: the group was created but one or more `add_member` calls failed partway through (`detail=partial_materialisation added=<X> failed=<Y>`) — the group is left existing with only the members that actually landed; there is no rollback, so a SIEM rule on `result=failure` here should treat the named group as needing manual review, not as a failed no-op. Permission gate: `ManagementGroup:Write`; the id-list materialised is additionally intersected against the caller's `Response:Read`-visible agent set before creation (see the `response.read`/`denied` row above for the corresponding drop-audit). |
| `management_group.assign_role` | ManagementGroup | A role is assigned to a principal on a management group |
| `management_group.unassign_role` | ManagementGroup | A role is unassigned from a principal on a management group |
| `api_token.create` | ApiToken | An API token is generated |
| `api_token.revoke` | ApiToken | An API token is revoked |
| `api_token.rotate` | ApiToken | A human token's self-service overlap-pair rotation was initiated (`POST /api/v1/tokens/{id}/rotate` or the `rotate_api_token` MCP twin, P2 #11, SOC 2 CC6.3) — the human twin of `engine_principal.credential.rotate`, self-service rather than admin-gated (`ApiToken:Rotate`, owner-vs-nonexistent 404 belt: the caller must own `{id}`). `target_id` is the **predecessor's** token id — the token being rotated, not the successor it mints. `result=denied` when `{id}` exists but is owned by a different principal (`detail=owner=<principal_id>`, same body as a genuine 404 so the route is not an ownership-enumeration oracle); `result=failure` when the store rejects the rotation (e.g. below the 24h overlap floor, or a different operator touching an in-flight rotation; `detail` is the store error). `result=partial` is a THIRD, distinct outcome (UP-11): `rotate_token` already committed and minted a live successor row, but reading its secret back to hand to the caller failed — the mint happened, only the reveal did not, so this is never folded into `failure` (which a CC6.3 reviewer would read as "no credential exists" when one plainly does). `result=success` is **not** emitted by this route — a successful rotation's evidence is the `api_token.reveal` row below, fired once the successor is confirmed findable. |
| `api_token.reveal` | ApiToken | The raw successor secret was returned to the caller by a human self-service rotate call — the human twin of `engine_principal.credential.reveal`, emitted on **every** successful reveal, including a same-caller grace-window re-serve of the same bytes. `target_id` is the **predecessor's** token id — the token that was rotated, NOT the successor whose secret this row records revealing; `api_token.confirm` stamps the successor's id instead, so the two ends of one rotation share no correlation key (#2970 C); `detail` is the literal string `rotate`. This row, not a `api_token.rotate` success row, is the audit evidence that a rotation actually delivered a secret. |
| `api_token.confirm` | ApiToken | A human self-service maker-checker confirmation that a rotation's successor secret was received/installed (`POST /api/v1/tokens/{id}/confirm` or the `confirm_api_token_rotation` MCP twin) — the human twin of `engine_principal.credential.confirm`; `{id}` is the successor token id the rotate response returned. `target_id` is the token id. `result=denied` when `{id}` exists but is owned by a different principal (`detail=owner=<principal_id>`, same 404-shaped body as rotate's denied case); `result=failure` when the store rejects the confirm (e.g. a replay after the rotation already resolved — `detail` carries the store's terminal message); `result=success` on confirm (`detail=confirmed`), which revokes the predecessor immediately and promotes the successor to the token's sole active credential. |
| `engine_principal.role.assigned` | EnginePrincipal | A fleet-wide RBAC role was assigned to an engine principal via `POST /api/v1/engine-principals/{id}/roles` or the MCP `assign_engine_role` twin (PR 4.2, design §4.1). `target_id` is the full `engine:<slug>` principal id. `result=success` with `detail=<role_name>`; `result=denied` (target is not a live engine principal, the role is unknown, or `validate_assignment` or the built-in-role check rejects an admin or built-in role — design §4.2 "no admin, ever"; a custom role that grants every permission is not refused here, the no-admin audit reports it) with `detail=<role_name>: <reason>`. Permission gate: `Security:Write`, plus admin + MFA step-up. |
| `engine_principal.role.unassigned` | EnginePrincipal | A fleet-wide RBAC role was removed from an engine principal via `DELETE /api/v1/engine-principals/{id}/roles/{role}` or the MCP `unassign_engine_role` twin. `target_id` is the full `engine:<slug>` principal id; `detail=<role_name>`. `result=success` only — the unassign is **idempotent** (matching the management-group sibling): removing a role the principal never held still returns `200` and still writes a `success` row, so a success row does not by itself prove a grant existed. A `503` (store unavailable) writes no row. Permission gate: `Security:Write`, plus admin + MFA step-up. |
| `engine_principal.role.listed` | EnginePrincipal | The fleet-wide RBAC roles assigned to an engine principal were enumerated via `GET /api/v1/engine-principals/{id}/roles` or the MCP `list_engine_roles` twin. `target_id` is the full `engine:<slug>` principal id; `result=success`. Audited because privilege enumeration is a security-sensitive read (SOC 2 CC7.2), even though it is a read. Permission gate: `EnginePrincipal:Read`. The Tokens and principals page fragments (`/fragments/access/principals/list` and `/detail`, gated on the same `EnginePrincipal:Read`) also write this verb, once per fragment and only when the roles were actually read (the list fragment for its Roles column, with an empty `target_id` and `detail=count=<rows>`; the detail fragment for the selected principal, `target_id` the principal id). A refused engine or MCP-tier session is recorded under `engine_principal.list` / `engine_principal.get` below, not here. |
| `engine_principal.create` | EnginePrincipal | A new engine-principal identity is minted via `POST /api/v1/engine-principals` or the `create_engine_principal` MCP tool. `detail` carries `owner=<username>; classification=<internal\|external>` on both REST and MCP. |
| `engine_principal.list` | EnginePrincipal | The engine-principal list surface (`GET /api/v1/engine-principals`) was enumerated. Emitted on every successful call, including by a read-only auditor role — an admit-then-audit read, not a mutation. `result=failure` is written by the REST route when the engine-principal list or the token store's credential counts could not be read (the route answers `503` and serves no list); `detail` is `engine principal roster could not be read` for the former and the token store's reason (`pool lease timed out`, `query failed` or `token store is not open`) for the latter. The row goes through the same connection pool that just failed, so it is best-effort: it can be lost, which REST signals with `Sec-Audit-Failed: true`. The Tokens and principals fragments write `result=success` when only a credential or role column renders `Unavailable`, and write no row when the roster itself cannot be read; the Settings Engine Principals panel writes no row at all. **REST-only** — the `list_engine_principals` MCP tool does not emit this verb; its call is recorded only under the generic `mcp.list_engine_principals` tool-call audit action (`target_type=mcp_tool`), not this domain-specific verb. The Tokens and principals page list fragment (`/fragments/access/principals/list`, gated on the same `EnginePrincipal:Read`) also writes this verb; an engine or MCP-tier session that passes the permission gate is then refused and writes `result=denied`, `detail=engine_session_denied` or `mcp_session_denied` (one that fails the permission gate gets that gate's ordinary denial). |
| `engine_principal.get` | EnginePrincipal | A single engine principal's identity row was read (`GET /api/v1/engine-principals/{id}`). `result=failure` is written by the REST route when the token store's credential read failed (the route answers `503` and serves no `active_credentials`); `detail` is the store's reason (`pool lease timed out`, `query failed` or `token store is not open`). The MCP tool's call is recorded as the generic `mcp.get_engine_principal` row with `result=failure`, `detail=principal_id=<id> <reason>` (best-effort, as above). The Tokens and principals detail fragment writes `result=success` when only its credentials render `Unavailable`. **REST-only**, same caveat as `engine_principal.list` above — the `get_engine_principal` MCP tool records only the generic `mcp.get_engine_principal` tool-call audit action. The Tokens and principals page detail fragment (`/fragments/access/principals/detail`, gated on the same `EnginePrincipal:Read`) also writes this verb; an engine or MCP-tier session that passes the permission gate is then refused and writes `result=denied`, `detail=engine_session_denied` or `mcp_session_denied` (one that fails the permission gate gets that gate's ordinary denial). |
| `engine_principal.revoke` | EnginePrincipal | An engine principal was terminally revoked (`DELETE /api/v1/engine-principals/{id}` or `revoke_engine_principal`). `detail` carries `credentials_revoked=<N>` and, if given, `superseded_by=<successor id>` (REST) or the caller-supplied `reason` (MCP). The revoke call itself is idempotent (a repeat call on an already-revoked principal still returns success, never an error) but **the audit row is not** — a repeat call **re-emits** a fresh `result=success` row each time (with `credentials_revoked=0` on the repeat), so the audit trail shows every repeat operator action, not just the first revoke. |
| `engine_principal.credential.mint` | EnginePrincipal | The first credential was minted for an engine principal (`POST /api/v1/engine-principals/{id}/credentials` or `mint_engine_credential`). **REST:** `target_id` is the engine principal id; `detail` carries `ttl_days=<N>`. **MCP:** `target_id` is instead the newly-minted credential's `token_id` (a success row always has it: a read-back that finds no row is a `partial` row, below); `detail` carries `principal=<engine principal id>`, not `ttl_days`. The raw secret itself is never written to the audit log on either surface. `result=failure` is also written, on REST and MCP, when the check for an existing active credential could not read the token store (the call answers `503` / a retryable error and mints nothing; `detail` is the store error). `result=partial` means the credential was minted but the read that locates it for the response failed, or answered and found no active credential (revoked out of band): the secret was withheld and no one holds it, so list the principal's credentials and rotate if one is listed rather than retrying the mint (`target_id` is the principal id in that case; `detail` says which of the two it was). On MCP a `partial` or `failure` outcome also writes an `mcp.mint_engine_credential` row. |
| `engine_principal.credential.rotate` | EnginePrincipal | An overlap-pair credential rotation was initiated or continued (`POST /api/v1/engine-principals/{id}/credentials/rotate` or `rotate_engine_credential`). `result=failure` on a rejected call (e.g. below the 24h overlap floor, or a different operator touching an in-flight rotation); on success the reveal itself is recorded separately as `engine_principal.credential.reveal` below, not folded into this row. `target_id` is the engine principal id on both REST and MCP. `result=partial` (REST and MCP) means the rotation committed and a successor exists (newly minted or re-served), but reading it back to build the response failed, or no active credential linked back to a predecessor: the call answered `503` / a retryable error, the secret was withheld, and no `reveal` row follows, so it is never recorded as `failure`. The no-linked-successor case carries no retry hint (a retry would perform a new rotation), unlike the read-failure case. On MCP a `partial` outcome also writes an `mcp.rotate_engine_credential` row. A `partial` row is the operator signal that a successor exists whose secret was never delivered; recover per [Engine Principals](engine-principals.md#3-rotate-the-credential-overlap-pair-model). Otherwise this verb is emitted only on the failure path. |
| `engine_principal.credential.reveal` | EnginePrincipal | The raw successor secret was returned to the caller by a rotate call, emitted on **every** successful reveal, including a same-caller grace-window re-serve of the same bytes, so every time a secret left the server is independently on the audit chain. **REST:** `target_id` is the engine principal id; `detail` is the literal string `rotate`. **MCP:** `target_id` is instead the successor credential's `token_id` (a rotation whose successor cannot be read back returns earlier as a partial and writes no reveal row); `detail` is `principal=<engine principal id> action=rotate`. |
| `engine_principal.credential.confirm` | EnginePrincipal | A rotation's successor secret was explicitly confirmed received/installed by its consumer (`POST /api/v1/engine-principals/{id}/credentials/confirm` or `confirm_engine_rotation`), closing the overlap window early and revoking the predecessor. A separate attestation from the reveal — never inferred from a successful rotate call. `target_id` is the engine principal id; on success `detail` carries `token_id=<confirmed successor id>` on both REST and MCP (#2384 — server-verified against the pending pair, never a raw secret), binding the attestation to the exact credential confirmed. A **replay after the rotation already resolved** writes a `failure` row here whose `detail` is the terminal store message (`rotation already confirmed ...` / `no rotation in flight ...` / `the rotation was resolved ...`, #2404) — the forensic evidence that a client kept confirming a resolved rotation, paired with the `yuzu_engine_principal_confirm_total{result="conflict"}` signal. (Audit-persist failure on a **mutating** engine-principal REST route — this row included — now FAILS THE REQUEST CLOSED: `503` + `Sec-Audit-Failed: true`, since #2466/#2406, so the privileged mutation never reports success on an unrecorded row; **since #3937 the MCP mutation twins fail closed too** — a JSON-RPC `503` error with `audit_persisted:false` in `error.data` (not on a success result). The engine-principal **reads** instead set `Sec-Audit-Failed: true` and proceed — they commit no state and disclose only authorization metadata, not per-person PII. See `docs/user-manual/rest-api.md` "Engine Principals".) |
| `engine_principal.transfer_owner` | EnginePrincipal | An engine principal's named responsible owner was reassigned (`POST /api/v1/engine-principals/{id}/transfer-owner` or `transfer_engine_principal_owner`). Admin-forced — does not depend on the outgoing owner's cooperation. **REST:** `detail` carries `old_owner=<username>; new_owner=<username>`. **MCP:** `detail` carries only `new_owner=<username>` — the outgoing owner is not named in the MCP audit row. |
| `engine_principal.audit.no_admin` | AuditLog | The "no admin" auditor ran (`GET /api/v1/engine-principals/audit/no-admin` or `audit_engine_no_admin`). `target_id` is the literal string `no-admin`; on `result=success` `detail` carries `violations=<N> scope=all` (`scope=active` when the MCP tool was narrowed with `include_revoked=false`) and, when N is above zero, `sample=` followed by up to ten comma-separated `principal_id:reason` pairs. `result=failure` (not `denied`) with `detail=rbac_resolution_failed` when the run could not verify (a grants, roles or role-permissions read failed, or the RBAC reference data was unresolvable); no `success` row is written for that run, and that outcome must never be read as "clean". The rows are the evidence the control operated: the auditor is pull-only, so a period with no rows shows at most that no recorded run exists, and is not evidence that the check ran or that it passed (a dropped success-row write is signalled only by `Sec-Audit-Failed` on REST; the MCP tool gives no signal). The Tokens page also runs it incidentally, in the browser, when a viewer who holds `AuditLog:Read` loads the principals panel; that is not a schedule and not a control. |
| `engine_principal.rotation.auto_revoke` | ApiToken | The 60-second background overlap-rotation sweep auto-revoked a predecessor credential whose overlap window elapsed without an explicit `confirm`. `principal="system"`; `detail` carries `principal_id=<engine principal> reason=overlap_window_elapsed`. **Governance UP-5:** the sweep withholds this auto-revoke (writes no row) when the successor was never presented at all (`last_used_at == 0`) — a dropped/lost successor secret must never leave the principal at zero usable credentials; the pair stays fully active and `.successor_unused` below is raised instead (see that row for its cadence — the audit row fires once per pair per state, not per tick). Also capped per tick (`kMaxAutoRevokesPerTick`) — a clock jump drains over several ticks rather than auto-revoking the whole eligible set in one; a capped tick logs and increments `yuzu_rotation_sweep_capped_total` (deliberately kind-neutral — shared across engine and human rotation pairs) but writes no distinct audit row of its own. |
| `engine_principal.rotation.successor_unused` | ApiToken | The same background sweep's operational health signal: a rotation's successor credential is nearing its predecessor's overlap-expiry with no recorded use. **Operational, not security** — `result=warning`, deliberately not folded into `event="security"`. Fires once per rotation attempt pre-elapse, then once more on crossing into the elapsed state (the pair the `.auto_revoke` carve-out above is now deliberately leaving alone) — the audit row and its metric are de-duplicated **once per pair per state**, process-local (a restart re-emits once). The underlying log line, by contrast, repeats every tick once the window has elapsed, so a stuck-open rotation is never silent even though the audit row and metric fire only once per state. `detail` carries `principal_id=<...> predecessor_token_id=<...> overlap_expires_at=<epoch>`, plus `status=elapsed_unrevoked_successor_unused` once past the window. |
| `api_token.rotation.auto_revoke` | ApiToken | The human-owned twin of `engine_principal.rotation.auto_revoke` above (P2 #11, SOC 2 CC6.3) — the SAME 60-second background overlap-rotation sweep auto-revoked a HUMAN predecessor token whose overlap window elapsed without an explicit confirm. Routed here instead of the engine action by the swept row's own `principal_kind` (the single chokepoint `rotation_sweep_names_for_kind`, `rotation_sweep_naming.hpp`), never by which endpoint minted the pair. `principal="system"`; `detail` carries `principal_id=<username> reason=overlap_window_elapsed`. Same UP-5 never-used-successor carve-out and per-tick cap as the engine row above. |
| `api_token.rotation.successor_unused` | ApiToken | The human-owned twin of `engine_principal.rotation.successor_unused` above (P2 #11) — same background sweep's operational health signal for a HUMAN rotation pair: the successor token is nearing its predecessor's overlap-expiry with no recorded use. **Operational, not security** — `result=warning`. Fires once per rotation attempt pre-elapse, then once more on crossing into the elapsed state (same UP-5 behavior as the engine row above) — the audit row and its metric are de-duplicated once per pair per state, process-local (a restart re-emits once); the underlying log line repeats every tick once elapsed. `detail` carries `principal_id=<...> predecessor_token_id=<...> overlap_expires_at=<epoch>`, plus `status=elapsed_unrevoked_successor_unused` once past the window. |
| `engine_principal.owner_deprovisioned` | EnginePrincipal | A SCIM deprovision (deactivate/delete) removed a user who still owned active engine principals. The deprovision itself **always succeeds** (a CC6.8 termination is never blocked) — this is a *detective*, not preventive, control: it also increments `yuzu_engine_principal_owner_deprovisioned_total` so an admin can reassign ownership out of band. `target_id` is the departing username; `detail` carries `orphaned_active_engine_principals=<N>` or `engine_ownership_unverifiable`. Never fires on a SCIM undo/rollback. The interactive dashboard delete uses the preventive form instead (blocked with `409`). |
| `quarantine.enable` | Security | A device is placed in quarantine. **A request the REST route refuses at the dispatch approval check (#6065) writes `result=denied` with `detail` `reason=<label> securable=<name>` (`target_id` is the agent id) and NO `attempt` row, because nothing was attempted (the `denied` row is best-effort: a failed write is logged but does not change the `403`, since no state changed); a refusal by the scoped permission gate before that check is audited by that gate, not here. An unwired check writes `result=failure`.** **Every row the REST route handler writes after its first look-up is stamped from the session the route resolved, not from a second look-up of the request, so the actor on the `attempt` and outcome rows is the account recorded as `quarantined_by`. Not covered: rows the scoped permission gate writes itself, and the `result=failure` row with `detail` `audit identity writer not configured` that an unwired writer produces (the route also answers `500`), which come from a look-up of the request. A request whose two look-ups name different principals ends at `401` with a `result=denied` row naming the first principal, `detail` `authenticated principal changed during the request now=<second account>` (the name has control bytes, spaces, commas, `=` and `;` replaced by `_` and is cut at 128 bytes), and writes no record; like the approval-check `denied` row it is best-effort.** **The REST route writes an `attempt` row (`result=attempt`) after authorisation and before the change, then `success` or `failure`; if the `attempt` row cannot be written it answers `503` with `Sec-Audit-Failed: true` and changes nothing, and a lost outcome row is flagged with the same header. `success` therefore always means the change was applied.** **Caveat for evidence queries:** the MCP twin `quarantine_device` — the only surface that both records *and* dispatches isolation — audits under `action="mcp.quarantine_device"` with `target_type="mcp_tool"` and the agent id in free-text `detail`, following the `mcp_audit` convention shared by every MCP tool. So `?target_type=Security&target_id=<agent>` returns the REST rows and the `quarantine.dispatch_denied` rows but **not** the MCP ones; add `?action=mcp.quarantine_device` and match the agent id in `detail` to complete the picture for a device. |
| `quarantine.disable` | Security | A device is released from quarantine. Written like `quarantine.enable`: an `attempt` row before the change, then `success` or `failure`; if the `attempt` row cannot be written the REST route answers `503` with `Sec-Audit-Failed: true` and changes nothing. **A request the REST route refuses at the dispatch approval check (#6065) writes `result=denied` with `detail` `reason=<label> securable=<name>` and NO `attempt` row; the record stays active.** Rows are stamped from the session the route resolved, as for `quarantine.enable`, and the changed-principal `401` is written here as `result=denied` with the `detail` given there. |
| `quarantine.dispatch_denied` | Security | An instruction dispatch was **refused** because the containment gate would not let it through (#881). `result=denied` always. **Three** shapes, distinguished by `detail` — and `target_id="*"` means two different things, so read `detail` before interpreting it. **(1) Per-device:** `QUARANTINE_DISPATCH_DENIED command=<id> agent=<id> reason=active_quarantine`, `target_id` = the contained device, one row each. **(2) Fail-closed:** `reason=degraded_fail_closed agents=<N>`, `target_id=*` — ONE row for the whole dispatch, because a fail-closed denial is a single policy decision about every connected agent and N synchronous audit INSERTs would storm the very pool whose degradation caused it. **(3) Capped fan-out:** `reason=active_quarantine agents_elided=<N> agents_total=<M>`, `target_id=*` — emitted after the first **25** per-device rows when one dispatch is denied against more than 25 contained devices. Shape-1 rows for that dispatch still exist; this row declares how many did not get one. **Which 25 get rows is unspecified** (the head of the target list in registry order — an arbitrary, unstable sample), and for a broadcast the elided identities are not reconstructable, since no target list is stored. The cap exists because the alternative is unbounded: a 1,000-host containment incident plus a per-minute scheduled broadcast is ~1.44M rows/day of blocking INSERTs on the dispatch thread; capped it is ~37k. In **every** shape the paired `yuzu_server_dispatch_target_rejected_total{reason="quarantined"}` counter moves by the TRUE denial count — count denials there, and use these rows for identity. The quarantine plugin's own control actions (`quarantine`/`unquarantine`/`status`/`whitelist`) are exempt from the gate and never produce this row — release must not be blocked by the containment it lifts. |
| `quarantine.reapply` | Security | A **system-initiated** re-application of endpoint containment for a device that reconnected while an active quarantine record existed (#3425), distinct from the operator-initiated `quarantine.enable` above. `principal="system"`. `target_id` is the device. Four shapes, distinguished by `result`/`detail`: **(1)** `result=success`, `detail=record_id=<id> command_id=<id> trigger=tick\|heartbeat` — the reconciler re-dispatched the stored whitelist and the plugin registry accepted it (not yet proof of endpoint containment). **(2)** `result=success`, `detail=CONFIRMED record_id=<id> command_id=<id>` — a follow-up `quarantine.status` read reported `state\|active`, the only signal this repo treats as proof the endpoint firewall is actually applied. This is the target agent's own self-report of its own containment state (cert-bound to the correct agent identity, so a DIFFERENT agent cannot forge it — but not independently corroborated by any network-side signal), the same trust boundary #3127's MCP `already_active` retry path already relies on for its own confirmation check; see `docs/user-manual/security-hardening.md`'s "Reconnect re-application" section. **(3)** `result=failure`, `detail=<what> record_id=<id> command_id=<id> but the durable stamp failed: <store error>` — the dispatch WAS accepted (or the status read DID confirm `state\|active`), a real containment-affecting action already happened, but the durable `last_applied_at`/`last_confirmed_at` stamp write failed (a store outage, or a concurrent release/requarantine racing the write) — recorded so a transient store failure never produces a silent gap in exactly the evidence this verb exists to provide. **(4)** `result=failure`, `detail=status read confirmed state\|active but the session churned between dispatch and confirm (dispatch_session=<id> confirm_session=<id>) record_id=<id> command_id=<id>` — the status read DID say `state\|active`, but the session it describes is not the session live now (a reboot landed between the reconciler sending the verifying dispatch and processing the response), so the read is refused as confirmation evidence: no durable stamp write is even attempted (unlike shape (3), where the write was attempted and failed). This is the negative-case record for the reboot window the dispatch-time session check closes — refused one step before the write is ever attempted, distinct from shape (3)'s two write-failure call sites (one of which is unrelated to session churn entirely — a dispatch-accepted-but-store-write-failed outcome can happen with no reboot involved). `record_id` is the internal `QuarantineRecord::id` (not exposed on REST/MCP) the reapply cycle was built from — added so a release-then-requarantine race for the same `agent_id` leaves an unambiguous audit trail across the two quarantine episodes, matching the record-identity scoping the store write itself already applies. A re-application that never reaches a dispatch attempt at all (device offline, stored whitelist invalid, degraded read BEFORE any dispatch) is still counted via `yuzu_server_quarantine_reapply_total{result}` rather than audited, the same "counted, not audited" posture `yuzu_server_system_reserved_push_total` documents for its own bypass channels, chosen for the identical reason: an unreachable device produces this outcome on every tick for as long as it stays offline, and an audit row per tick per offline device is an unbounded write rate the counter already answers without it. Shapes (3) and (4) are the exceptions — a genuine agent-facing action, or a genuine agent self-report, already happened, so neither is merely counted. |
| `policy.evaluate` | Policy | A forced compliance check was triggered via `POST /api/policies/{id}/evaluate`. `target_id` is the policy id; on `result=success` `detail` is `execution_id=<id>`. The `409` branch (no check instruction / no matching agents) rejects without an audit row. Permission gate: `Policy:Execute`. |
| `policy.remediate` | Policy | A manual remediation was triggered via `POST /api/policies/{id}/remediate`. `result` is `success`, `denied`, or `error`. On success: `detail` is `execution_id=<id> agents=<N>`. On denied: `detail` is the rejection reason (no fix instruction / no in-scope agents / no non_compliant agents / a remediation for this policy is already in flight). On error: `detail` is a genuine store/evaluator degrade — distinct from `denied`, which is always a business rejection. Remediation is always operator-initiated, never automatic. Permission gate: `Policy:Execute`. |
| `preflight.run` | Scope | An operator ran a `/auto` pre-flight check via `POST /fragments/auto/run`. `target_id` is the management-group id scoped to the run, or `all-visible` when no group filter was applied; `detail` carries `run=<id> checks=<N> devices=<N>`. `result=success`, or `no_devices` when the chosen scope resolved to no visible devices (a no-op, not a denial). **A service-scoped API token is denied outright** (`result=denied`, `target_id=` empty) before its device cohort is even resolved — the SEC-2/SEC-3 confinement-gap class: `devices_fn` is username-keyed and does not confine a service-scoped token, and unlike the read-only fixes elsewhere in this branch this route MUTATES (creates a run and dispatches the configured checks), so the deny is unconditional rather than data-dependent. **Two more emitters** share this same `result=denied` shape for the identical confinement-gap reason, applied to `session->username`-scoped reads rather than a fleet-wide list: `GET /fragments/auto` (the saved-runs rail) and `GET /fragments/auto/result` (the run result poll) — a service-scoped token shares its creating principal's username (`ApiToken::principal_id`), so without this deny it could enumerate or read back a fleet-wide run its own principal created interactively. Neither emits `result=success` (no dedicated success-path audit on the rail/poll — a lower-sensitivity read than the mutating run-creation path). Permission gates: `Infrastructure:Read` + `Execution:Execute` (run creation only; the rail/poll gate on `Infrastructure:Read` alone). |
| `preflight.run.delete` | Scope | An operator deleted a pre-flight run from the saved-runs rail via `POST /fragments/auto/delete?run=<id>`. `target_id` is the run id; `detail` carries `run=<id> scope=<label>`. `result=success` when an owned row was removed, `noop` when the run id was unknown or not owner-visible (a no-op rather than a permission rejection — prevents cross-owner id enumeration). **A service-scoped API token is denied outright** (`result=denied`, `target_id=` empty) before `delete_run` is ever called — same confinement-gap reason as the `preflight.run` rail/poll emitters above: a token sharing its creating principal's username could otherwise delete evidence for a fleet-wide run outside its own service. Permission gate: `Execution:Execute`; owner-scoped at the store seam (`created_by` must match). |
| `dex.app_perf.compare` | GuaranteedState | An operator ran a `/auto` **Verify** before/after app-performance **aggregate** comparison — REST `GET /api/v1/dex/perf/compare` or the dashboard VERIFY `/run` fragment. `target_id` is the cohort (management-group) id; `detail` carries `app=<name> base=<v> cand=<v> cohort=<N> paired=<N> view=aggregate cid=<cid>` (`paired=` so a singleton aggregate — effectively one machine's data — is distinguishable). **Operational, set-and-proceed**: the read is *recorded* (the accountability that stands in for the absent cohort floor — Verify deliberately has no minimum cohort size), but a dropped audit row sets the `Sec-Audit-Failed` response header rather than blocking the read. The aggregate carries **no per-machine identity**. Permission gate: `GuaranteedState:Read`; a service-scoped API token is denied outright (`result=denied`, `target_id=` empty) under this same verb, on both REST and the MCP tool `compare_app_perf_versions` — found by this branch's own governance review (PR #3156, while re-verifying the external review's separate findings on the same file): the route's own prior comment reasoned about RBAC management-group-scoped grants, a different axis from a service-scoped token, which holds the global `GuaranteedState:Read` grant via `ITServiceOwner` regardless and could otherwise supply any `group`. (Over MCP, a SUCCESSFUL `compare_app_perf_versions` call is recorded under the generic `mcp.compare_app_perf_versions` tool-call audit, not this verb — but its detail carries the same subject (`group=… app=… base=… cand=… cohort=… paired=…`), and a dropped row surfaces as `audit_persisted:false` in the result body since MCP has no `Sec-Audit-Failed` header. MCP exposes only the identity-free aggregate, no per-machine drill. The service-scoped DENY is the one case where MCP records under `dex.app_perf.compare` rather than the generic verb — matching the deny-path convention every other REST/MCP twin pair in this table uses.) |
| `dex.perf.group.view` | GuaranteedState | A service-scoped API token was denied outright (`result=denied`, `target_id=` empty) on `GET /api/v1/dex/perf/group` (REST), via that route's own explicit `deny_fleet_wide_service_scoped` call, which fires BEFORE `perm_fn` — found by this branch's own governance review (PR #3156), the same gap and reasoning as `dex.app_perf.compare` above. **MCP does not record this verb on the equivalent deny**: the MCP tool `get_dex_group_app_perf` used to have its own interim deny call here, but it was retired as provably dead code (guardian-confinement-2298 PR 3 "the flip", #3290 Phase 2 bucket 1a) — the SHARED `perm_fn` (every MCP tool-call branch runs behind it first) already denies any service-scoped token outright for `(GuaranteedState, Read)` before this tool-specific branch is ever reached, since the service-scope global-safe allow-list is seeded empty (`docs/adr/1006-service-scope-default-deny.md`). That denial is recorded under the generic `auth.permission_required` verb (`AuthRoutes::require_permission`'s service-scope branch, `auth_routes.cpp`), not under `dex.perf.group.view` or a `mcp.*` tool-call verb. This verb currently records the REST DENY only — a successful group app-perf-trend read is not audited on either transport (matches this route's existing aggregate/cohort posture: a management-group trend is treated the same as the unaudited fleet/app-level trends it sits beside). |
| `dex.perf.tag.view` | GuaranteedState | The device-model **tag-value cohort** twin of `dex.perf.group.view` just above — same gap, same reasoning, same MCP/REST asymmetry, applied to a device TAG value instead of a management-group id: a service-scoped API token is denied outright (`result=denied`, `target_id=` empty) on `GET /api/v1/dex/perf/tag` (REST), via that route's own explicit `deny_fleet_wide_service_scoped` call. **MCP does not record this verb on the equivalent deny**: `get_dex_tag_app_perf`'s own interim deny call was likewise retired as dead code (#3290 Phase 2 bucket 1a) — the shared `perm_fn` already denies every service-scoped token for `(GuaranteedState, Read)` before this tool-specific branch runs, and that denial is recorded under the generic `auth.permission_required` verb, not `dex.perf.tag.view` and not a `mcp.*` tool-call verb. A tag-value cohort is equally a "named set of specific devices" for confinement purposes. A successful tag-cohort trend read is not audited on either transport, matching `dex.perf.group.view`'s existing posture. |
| `dex.app_perf.compare.drill` | GuaranteedState | An operator opened the **per-machine pairs** of a `/auto` Verify comparison (the dashboard `/fragments/auto/verify/drill`, reached by a deliberate "Show per-machine pairs" click). This is the per-device behavioural-PII view, so it carries its **own** verb (distinct from the identity-free aggregate above) to keep per-machine access independently countable. `target_id` is the cohort id; `detail` carries `app=<name> base=<v> cand=<v> cid=<cid>`. Operational set-and-proceed (the HTML surface renders even on a lost audit row, setting `Sec-Audit-Failed`). There is **no REST or MCP** twin of the drill — the per-machine pairs are dashboard-only. Permission gate: `GuaranteedState:Read`. |
| `dex.app_perf.devices.view` | GuaranteedState | An operator (or integration) opened the **version-row "which devices" drill** on the application-performance-over-time trend — devices reporting one exact `(app, version)` among their retained top-N daily summaries, each row an `agent_id` + last-seen day + that day's CPU/working set. `target_id=` empty (fleet-wide, filtered to the caller's own visibility rather than a single entity); `detail` carries `app=<name> version=<token> devices=<n> cid=<cid>` — the count is recorded AFTER the read so it is honest, but the row is written BEFORE the response is composed. Permission gate: `GuaranteedState:Read` via `require_fleet_read` (ADR-0017) — unlike `dex.perf.device.view` below, a management-group/service-scoped caller is admitted with the visible-agent intersection pushed into the store query, not denied outright, because the underlying store query can genuinely narrow per-caller (routed-concerns.md's DEX row has the full reasoning). **Two emitters, different postures:** the REST endpoint (`GET /api/v1/dex/perf/app/devices`) fails closed (`503` + `Sec-Audit-Failed: true`) if the row cannot persist; the MCP tool `list_dex_app_perf_devices` is set-and-proceed (`audit_persisted:false` in the result body on a dropped row). No dashboard-fragment success audit failure blanks the page either (same set-and-proceed posture, via `try_persist_audit`). |
| `deployment.config.view` | Scope | A service-scoped API token was denied outright (`result=denied`, `target_id=` empty) on the deploy config form, `GET /fragments/auto/deploy?run=<id>`, before its source pre-flight run is even resolved — the SEC-2/SEC-3 confinement-gap class: `PreflightRunStore::get_run` is owner-scoped by `session->username` alone, and a service-scoped token shares its creating principal's username (`ApiToken::principal_id`), so without this deny it could read back the go/warn counts for a fleet-wide run outside its own service. No success-path emission (a lower-sensitivity config-recap read than deployment creation itself). Permission gate: `SoftwareDeployment:Read`. |
| `deployment.create` | SoftwareDeployment | An operator deployed an installer to a pre-flight run's go-cohort via `POST /fragments/auto/deploy/run`. `target_id` is the new `deployment_id`; `detail` carries `run=<source_run_id> file=<filename> sha256=<hash> url=<url> devices=<N>` — the **SHA-256 is the content identity of the binary executed on the fleet**. `result=success` (deployment created + first advance), `resumed` (an in-flight deployment for this run already existed — re-attached, not re-created, so the installer is not run twice; `target_id` is the existing deployment), or `no_devices` (the go-cohort was empty after re-intersecting with the operator's current visible set — a no-op). **A service-scoped API token is denied outright** (`result=denied`, `target_id=` empty) before the run's go-cohort is even resolved — this is the FIRST fleet-wide dispatch step for a deployment, the same confinement-gap class as `deployment.advance`/`.delete` below (found by external review, PR #3156, after those two siblings were fixed). Permission gates: `Infrastructure:Read` + `SoftwareDeployment:Execute`. |
| `deployment.advance` | SoftwareDeployment | The deployment engine was ticked via the result-poll `GET /fragments/auto/deploy/result`. Emitted on **every poll tick** while the page is open (up to ~600 ticks before the page stops self-polling); `result=success`. `target_id` is the `deployment_id`. SIEM: a burst of these rows with one `deployment_id` is a single open-page session, not repeated operator intent (auditing only ticks that actually dispatch is a tracked follow-up). **A service-scoped API token is denied outright** (`result=denied`, `target_id=` empty) before the tick ever runs — same confinement-gap reason as `deployment.config.view` above: the token shares its creating principal's username, and this route re-invokes the engine's mutating advance() (claims + dispatches to newly-authorized devices). Permission gates: `SoftwareDeployment:Read` + `SoftwareDeployment:Execute`. |
