| `execution.detail.fetch` | Execution | Was fully distinct from `execution.read` above (that verb covered only the legacy `/api/executions/*` family; this one covered the v1 twins, #4030) - **no longer true as of #2146 A2-R1**: the new REST v1 `GET /api/v1/executions/{id}/children` twin emits `execution.read` on denial (see that row above), not this verb, so the split is now per-ROUTE, not per-transport-generation. `GET /api/v1/executions/{id}?include=agents` (per-agent status/duration expansion) and `GET /api/v1/executions/{id}/responses` both write `result=success` rows under this verb on their 200 path (response body/agent-identity disclosure); the bare `GET /api/v1/executions/{id}` (no `include`) does not audit its 200 path at all, only `result=denied` on the 404/scope-denial branch, matching `execution.read`'s no-oracle shape (identical detail text whether nonexistent or out-of-scope). **A third, unaudited branch (#2146 A2-R1 governance fix):** a transient tracker degrade (pool/query failure distinguished via `get_execution_checked`) on the bare detail route now answers 503 and writes NO row at all under this verb, either `success` or `denied` - do not mistake a quiet period on this verb for "no denials occurred," since a degrade window also produces silence here. Both audited call sites REST fail-closed (503 + `Sec-Audit-Failed: true`) on an audit-persist failure — do not confuse with `workflow_execution.detail.fetch` below, a set-and-proceed posture. Permission gate: `require_fleet_read("Execution","Read")` on the detail route, `require_fleet_read("Response","Read")` on `/responses`. |
| `workflow_execution.detail.fetch` | WorkflowExecution | `GET /api/v1/workflow-executions/{id}` and MCP `get_workflow_execution` (#4030) — WorkflowEngine's own multi-step run record, a different data model from `Execution`. `result=success` on an admitted read; `result=denied` (#4030 Gate 8 fix) on a confined caller with no agent visible in the execution's `agent_ids` — the denied row's detail text is non-distinguishing (identical whether the execution is out-of-scope or would otherwise 404), but **unlike `execution.read`/`execution.detail.fetch` above, a genuinely nonexistent id writes no row at all**: the mere presence of a `denied` row for a given id is therefore itself an existence signal to a caller who also holds `AuditLog:Read` (narrow, existence-only; tracked open, not closed by this fix round). **Set-and-proceed, NOT fail-closed** — this file's `AuditFn`/MCP path predates the checked-bool `try_persist_audit`/`emit_behavioral_audit` helpers `execution.detail.fetch` above uses; an audit-persist failure on an admitted read does not block it (MCP surfaces `audit_persisted:false` on the response payload when it happens; REST has no equivalent signal). Permission gate: `require_fleet_read("Workflow","Read")`. |
| `ota.package.uploaded` | UpdatePackage | An admin uploaded an agent OTA update binary via Settings → OTA Updates. `target_id` is `<platform>/<arch>/<version>`; `detail` carries `file=<name> sha256=<hex> signed=true\|false rollout=<N>%`. **`signed=` is the field to alert on.** Re-uploading a package with the Signature field left empty removes the previous detached signature, silently downgrading that package from signed to unsigned for the entire fleet — a `signed=false` row for a package that was previously `signed=true` is that transition, and it is the only durable record of it. The SHA-256 is the content identity of the binary every endpoint will execute. |
| `ota.package.rollout_changed` | UpdatePackage | An admin changed the rollout percentage of an agent OTA update package via Settings → OTA Updates. `target_id` is `<platform>/<arch>/<version>`; `detail` carries `from=<N>% to=<M>% mandatory=true\|false`. **The `from=` value is the point of this row**, and it is the value the write actually replaced — the row is read under a lock and updated in the same database transaction, so a concurrent change to the same package cannot land between the two and leave `from=` naming a value that was never replaced. A rollout of 0% cannot, on its own, distinguish a mandatory security patch that was deliberately pulled back from one that was never rolled out — `from=100% to=0% mandatory=true` can, and that is the scenario this audit exists to make reconstructable. The percentage is clamped to 0–100 server-side and the row reports the **clamped, stored** value, not the requested one. **Three outcomes, and only one of them is evidence of a change.** `result=success` — the package was found and the write committed; `detail` carries `from=`/`to=`. `result=denied` with `detail=not_found` — the store reported the package as absent, so nothing changed; this is the same shape the file's other rejection branches use, and the `result == "denied"` filter under **Result vocabulary** surfaces it along with every other probe. `result=failure` — the change did NOT take effect, for one of two reasons named in `detail`: `outcome=write_not_committed` (the package was found but the registry write did not commit, so `detail` carries `attempted_from=`/`attempted_to=` rather than `from=`/`to=`), or `outcome=store_unavailable existence_unknown=true` (the registry could not be read at all). **The second is deliberately NOT `denied`:** the store's reads fail soft, returning an empty result on a degrade, so treating that as absence would make a database blip both manufacture enumeration alerts and assert in the evidence record that a package which exists did not. |
| `ota.package.deleted` | UpdatePackage | An admin deleted an OTA update package. `target_id` is `<platform>/<arch>/<version>`; the binary **and** its signature sidecar are both removed. `result=success` when a matching package was found and both files were dealt with cleanly, with `detail` naming each outcome separately — `binary removed, signature sidecar removed`, or `... already absent` for either half where the file was not there to begin with (a package whose binary was removed out of band is a different fact from one this call deleted). `result=partial` when the registry row was removed but a filesystem delete **failed**: `detail=registry row removed; binary delete failed: <reason>` (and/or the sidecar equivalent). The package stops being served either way — the row is gone — but the leftover file is still on disk and needs manual removal, and this row is the only record of that. A delete naming a package that does not exist removes nothing and records `result=denied` with `detail=not_found` — the same shape this file's other rejection branches use, so the `result == "denied"` filter described under **Result vocabulary** surfaces it along with every other probe. |
| `plugin_signing.bundle.uploaded` | PluginTrustBundle | An admin uploaded a PEM trust bundle via Settings → Plugin Code Signing. `detail` carries `<N> cert(s), sha256=<hex>` on success. On `result=failure` (PEM validation rejected the input — empty body, missing markers, no parsable cert, hashing failure, etc.) `detail` carries the validation error string. Audit row is emitted on both branches so a SIEM rule on `failure` can detect malformed-upload probing. |
| `plugin_signing.bundle.cleared` | PluginTrustBundle | An admin removed the trust bundle (Settings → Plugin Code Signing → Remove). `detail` carries `file removed` (a file existed and was deleted) or `no file present` (clear was a no-op). Always emits `success` unless the require-flag DB write fails first, in which case `result=failure` and `detail` carries the store error — and the file is **not** removed (two-phase commit prevents the disk/DB-flag desync). |
| `plugin_signing.require.changed` | RuntimeConfig | An admin toggled "Require signed plugins" via Settings. `target_id` is `plugin_signing_required`, `detail` carries the new value (`"true"` or `"false"`). Emitted on every change including no-op idempotent re-saves. |
| `plugin_config.kill_switch.set` | PluginConfig | An operator flipped a plugin's dispatch kill switch via `PUT /api/v1/plugin-config/{plugin}/kill-switch` or the MCP `set_plugin_kill_switch` (`PluginConfig:Write`) — how a default-off plugin such as `privacy_permissions` is enabled. `target_id` is `<plugin>` (`<plugin>.<action>` for an action-scoped switch, with an `@<os>` suffix for a per-OS switch); `detail` is `enabled=true|false`, the requested value, not the prior one, with `, protective_exempt=true` appended when the row covers a protective pair (the quarantine plugin's four actions or `__guard__.push_rules`): the row is stored but never gates dispatch (see [Upgrading](upgrading.md#behaviour-change-protective-actions-exempt-from-plugin-enablement)); when the caller gave a `reason` it follows as `, reason="<text>"` (at most 512 bytes before escaping; the server refuses a reason containing control characters other than tab, and tab is flattened to a space; `\` and `"` are backslash-escaped; a reason over the limit is refused (400 on REST, invalid-params on MCP) before anything is written, so a row does not normally carry a cut reason; as a backstop the audit writer cuts at a character boundary and appends `, reason_truncated=true` after the closing quote; match that marker only after the closing quote, since a reason may itself contain the same text) on both rows, because the stored switch keeps only its latest reason. Two rows per call: `result=attempted` is written BEFORE the change (if it cannot be persisted the call is refused with 503 and the switch is unchanged), then `result=success` or `result=failure`. |
| `response_template.create` | InstructionDefinition | A response-view template was created via `POST /api/v1/definitions/{id}/response-templates` (issue #254 / Phase 8.2). `target_id` is the definition id; `detail` is the new template id on success, or `reason=<r>` on the audit path. RBAC denials, 400 (malformed id / invalid JSON / validation), 404 (definition not found), and 413 (body too large) emit `result=denied`; 500 (persist failure) emits `result=failure`. Reasons: `malformed_definition_id`, `body_too_large`, `invalid_json`, `definition_not_found`, `validation_failed`, `persist_failure`. Permission gate: `InstructionDefinition:Write`. |
| `response_template.update` | InstructionDefinition | A response-view template was replaced via `PUT /api/v1/definitions/{id}/response-templates/{tid}` (issue #254). `target_id` is the definition id; `detail` is the template id on success, or `reason=<r>` on the audit path. 4xx branches emit `result=denied`; 500 persist failure emits `result=failure`. Reasons: `malformed_id`, `reserved_id`, `body_too_large`, `invalid_json`, `definition_not_found`, `template_not_found`, `validation_failed`, `persist_failure`. Permission gate: `InstructionDefinition:Write`. |
| `response_template.delete` | InstructionDefinition | A response-view template was removed via `DELETE /api/v1/definitions/{id}/response-templates/{tid}` (issue #254). `target_id` is the definition id; `detail` is the deleted template id on success, or `reason=<r>` on the audit path. 4xx branches emit `result=denied`; 500 persist failure emits `result=failure`. Reasons: `malformed_id`, `reserved_id`, `definition_not_found`, `template_not_found`, `persist_failure`. Permission gate: `InstructionDefinition:Write`. |
| `offload_target.create` | OffloadTarget | An offload target was created via `POST /api/v1/offload-targets` (issue #255 / Phase 8.3; ADR-0059). On success: `target_id` is the assigned numeric id (string-form), `detail` is `name=<n> url=<scheme>://<host>[:<port>] url_fp=<12 hex>` (the same shape as the delete row, so the record shows where data was sent; the REST route and the MCP `create_offload_target` tool write the same shape). The destination URL is a bearer secret, so the row never carries its path or query: `url_fp` is the first 12 hex characters of the URL's SHA-256, enough to tell whether two rows (or a row and a candidate URL) name the same destination and too little to recover it. Rows written by earlier builds carry the full `url=<u>` or the bare `name`, so a SIEM parser should accept all three. Those older rows are credential-like for as long as they are retained: rotate any Slack-style hook URL that appears in one at the provider, or record the decision to leave it. Each create also writes a `result=attempted` row BEFORE the target is stored (same `detail`, except `target_id` is the name made audit-safe, and only `url_fp=` is written when the URL has no plain `scheme://host[:port]`); if that row cannot be written the create is refused (REST `503` with `Sec-Audit-Failed: true`, MCP an error with a retry hint) and nothing is stored (ADR-0033 section 9). A cookie-session `POST` whose `Origin`/`Referer` names another site, or carries neither, is refused `403` and audited as `csrf.denied` (`target_id` is the route). On 400: `target_id` is the submitted name made audit-safe (as for `attempted`), `result=denied` with `detail` ∈ `validation_failed` (invalid URL scheme, embedded `user:password@`, `batch_size` outside 1 to 10000, control bytes in a free-text field, or duplicate name), `invalid_field_type` (a present-but-wrong-typed JSON field, e.g. `batch_size` sent as a string, float, boolean or null), or `invalid_auth_type` (an `auth_type` value outside `none`/`bearer`/`basic`/`hmac`). A missing or empty `name` or `url` is a `400` with no audit row. The MCP tool writes a denied row for a refused `batch_size` too: `invalid_field_type` for a wrong type or an integer above 9223372036854775807 (REST records `validation_failed` for that one value), and `validation_failed` for any other out-of-range integer, the latter only when `name` and `url` are present. A wrong-typed `batch_size` is audited even when `name` is missing, so that row can carry an empty `target_id`. On 503 (store degraded): `detail` ∈ `store_unavailable` (not open / pool exhausted) or `db_error` (a query against an open store failed) — distinct in the audit record even though both collapse to the same 503 HTTP status (#3097 classification). RBAC denial is emitted by the permission gate before this handler is reached. Permission gate: `Infrastructure:Write`. |
| `offload_target.delete` | OffloadTarget | An offload target was deleted via `DELETE /api/v1/offload-targets/{id}` (issue #255; ADR-0059). On success: `target_id` is the numeric id, `detail` is `name=<n> url=<scheme>://<host>[:<port>] url_fp=<12 hex>` (snapshotted before the delete so the record survives it, in the same non-secret form as `offload_target.create`; empty if that pre-delete snapshot read itself degraded), `result=success`. A `result=attempted` row with the same `detail` is written first; if it cannot be written the delete is refused (`503` + `Sec-Audit-Failed: true`) and the target stays. On 404 (id not found, or numeric overflow on the path segment, which has no `attempted` row): `detail=not_found`, `result=denied` — the operator's attempt is recorded so SIEMs can surface probing of non-existent offload targets. On 503 (store degraded): `detail` ∈ `store_unavailable`/`db_error`, same #3097 distinction as `offload_target.create` above. Permission gate: `Infrastructure:Write`. |
| `webhook.create` | Webhook | An operator created a webhook via `POST /api/webhooks` (the Integrations page uses this route). `target_id` is the new webhook id on `result=success`, empty on `failure`. `detail` is `url=<scheme>://<host>[:<port>] url_fp=<12 hex>` (`url=` is omitted when no host can be shown, see `offload_target.create`): the destination URL is a bearer secret, so the path and query are never recorded; older rows carry the full URL: rotate any hook URL seen in one at the provider, or record the decision to leave it. A `result=attempted` row (same `detail`) is written BEFORE the webhook is stored; if it cannot be written the create is refused (`503` + `Sec-Audit-Failed: true`) and nothing is stored. `result=failure` with `detail` `invalid_url` (400: not `http(s)`, embedded credentials or control bytes), `url_required`, `invalid_json`, `store_unavailable` or `db_error`. Permission gate: `Infrastructure:Write`. |
| `webhook.delete` | Webhook | An operator deleted a webhook via `DELETE /api/webhooks/{id}`. `target_id` is the id. `detail` is the pre-delete snapshot in the `webhook.create` form (empty if the snapshot read degraded), `result=success`; `result=failure` with `detail` `not_found` (404, including an id too large for a 64-bit integer, which has no `attempted` row), `store_unavailable` or `db_error`. A `result=attempted` row is written first; if it cannot be written the delete is refused (`503` + `Sec-Audit-Failed: true`). Permission gate: `Infrastructure:Write`. |
| `settings.mcp` | MCP | An administrator changed the MCP switches via `POST /api/settings/mcp`. `target_id` is `mcp_settings`; `detail` is `enabled=<bool>, read_only=<bool>`. A `result=attempted` row is written BEFORE the switches move, and if it cannot be written the change is refused (`503` + `Sec-Audit-Failed: true`); `result=success` follows. A malformed body (`enabled` and `read_only` must each appear once as `true` or `false`) is refused `400` with nothing changed and no row. A cookie-session post whose `Origin`/`Referer` names another site, or carries neither, is refused `403` (`csrf.denied`); token callers are not gated. |
| `csrf.denied` (integrations writes) | Endpoint | A browser (session-cookie) `POST`/`DELETE` to `/api/webhooks`, `/api/v1/offload-targets`, `POST /api/directory/sync`, `PUT /api/directory/group-mappings` or `POST /api/settings/mcp` that carried no `Origin`/`Referer`, or one naming another site, was refused with `403` before anything was parsed or written. `target_id` is the route. Token-authenticated callers are not gated; an `Origin` or `Referer` matching a `--csrf-trusted-origin` entry is accepted. For a cookie-session caller the `csrf.denied` (central gate) row is written instead. |
| `viz.fleet_topology` | FleetTopology | Fleet topology snapshot was requested via `GET /api/v1/viz/fleet/topology`, `GET /fragments/viz/fleet/topology` (PR 3 of feat/viz-engine ladder), or the MCP twin `get_fleet_topology` (api-parity #2146 Batch B3 — via the domain verb, not the generic `mcp.<tool>` action). `target_id` is empty (the action operates on the whole fleet). On success: `detail` is `machines=<N> include_vuln=<0|1> [fresh=1] [fragment=1] [offline_roster_degraded=1]` (REST) or `machines=<N> include_vuln=<0|1> [offline_roster_degraded=1] via MCP` (MCP), `result=success`; `offline_roster_degraded=1` is present when the durable offline-host read failed and the snapshot is live-only. On the `--viz-disable` kill switch: `detail=kill_switch[ via MCP]`, `result=denied`. On 413 (snapshot exceeds `machines_max`): `detail=oversize machines=<N> cap=<M>[ via MCP]`, `result=denied`. On 400 (`machines_max` malformed on REST — non-numeric / out-of-range / overflow, all mapped to `detail=bad_machines_max`; on MCP, a wrong-JSON-*type* `machines_max` is rejected by the handler's own strict type check (`param_int_strict`) before any audit call is made, but an in-range-type value that is merely out of `[1,100000]` reaches the same range check REST uses and is audited): `detail=bad_machines_max[ via MCP]`, `result=denied`. On internal error (store null / fetcher threw / null sentinel): `detail` is `store_null` / `fetch_threw` / `snap_null` (optionally suffixed ` via MCP`), `result=failure`. Permission gate: `Response:Read`. |
| `viz.fleet_topology.invalidate` | FleetTopology | The fleet topology cache was explicitly invalidated because `?fresh=1` (REST) or `fresh:true` (MCP `get_fleet_topology`) was passed on the request. Emitted as a separate row immediately before the corresponding `viz.fleet_topology` row so the operator-driven flush is its own evidence event. `target_id` empty, `detail` empty (REST) or `via MCP get_fleet_topology` (MCP), `result=success`. Permission gate: `Response:Read` (inherited from the parent request). |
| `viz.host_topology` | HostTopology | Per-host topology slice was requested via `GET /api/v1/viz/host/{id}/topology` or the MCP twin `get_host_topology` (api-parity #2146 Batch B3). `target_id` is the agent id. On the `--viz-disable` kill switch: `detail=kill_switch[ via MCP]`, `result=denied`. On success: `detail` is `fragment=1` or empty (REST) or `via MCP` (MCP), `result=success`. On not-found (agent id absent from the current live snapshot - no durable stale fallback here, unlike `viz.fleet_topology`): `detail=not_found[ via MCP]`, `result=failure` (an authorized-but-absent lookup, not a rejection). On internal error (store null / fetcher threw / null sentinel): `detail` is `store_null` / `fetch_threw` / `snap_null` (optionally suffixed ` via MCP`), `result=failure`. On MCP only: a missing/empty `agent_id` argument answers `kInvalidParams` with NO audit row at all (checked before the kill switch; REST cannot produce this state since `agent_id` is a required path segment). Permission gate: `Response:Read`. |
| `product_pack.install` | ProductPack | A product pack install was attempted via `POST /api/product-packs`. On success: `target_id` is the new pack id, `result=success`; `detail` is empty UNLESS the install was partial (#3479 — at least one document failed alongside ones that succeeded), in which case `detail` carries `"<failed>/<total> item(s) failed to install"` (the per-item reasons live in the response body's `errors` array, not the audit row). On rejection: `target_id` is empty (install failed pre-id-generation; pack name is attacker-controlled YAML and is deliberately not echoed into `target_id`), `detail` is the operator-facing error message, `result=denied`. Three rejection classes share `result=denied`: a **signature/policy rejection** (#802 / W7.4 — `"pack '<name>' is unsigned and signature enforcement is enabled (set --allow-unsigned-packs / ... to bypass)"` / `"signature verification failed for pack '<name>' — content may have been tampered with"` / `"pack '<name>' has signature but no publicKey — cannot verify"`), a **mid-install database failure** (store unreachable, or the pack/item persist transaction failed — `detail` carries a generic message, never the raw database error, ADR-0054; since #3481, when a compensation attempt actually ran — the real REST route always supplies `compensate_fn` — `detail` also carries a `(compensated N/M item(s))` suffix, same as the duplicate-item-id message below and in the REST response body, so an auditor can tell whether an orphan was left behind directly from the audit row), and, since #3481, an **idempotency-key conflict** (the same `Idempotency-Key` header reused with a different request body — `"idempotency key already used with a different request body"`). SOC 2 CC6.7: every request that reaches `ProductPackStore::install()` is logged regardless of outcome — this does NOT cover a request rejected before `install()` is ever called (an unparseable JSON body, the store being unavailable, or, since #3481, an oversized `Idempotency-Key` header): those return their error directly with no audit row, the same pre-existing convention this endpoint has always used for request-validation failures. Permission gate: `ProductPack:Write`. |
| `product_pack.uninstall` | ProductPack | A product pack uninstall was issued via `DELETE /api/product-packs/{id}`. `target_id` is the pack id. On success: `detail` is empty, `result=success`. On rejection (ADR-0054): `detail` is the operator-facing error message, `result=denied` — either the id does not exist, or a database failure occurred (store unreachable, or the item-delete transaction failed; `detail` carries a generic message in the latter case, never the raw database error). **Pre-migration, a rejected uninstall was not audited at all** — this closes that SOC 2 CC6.7 gap, mirroring `product_pack.install`'s own denied-row precedent. Permission gate: `ProductPack:Delete`. |
| `server.viz_disabled` | FleetTopology | Emitted ONCE at server startup when `--viz-disable` / `YUZU_VIZ_DISABLE=1` is set. `principal=system`, `target_id=viz`, `detail` describes the flag source, `result=success`. The matching `[VIZ] viz endpoint disabled by configuration` warn line in operator logs covers the same event; the audit row makes the disabled posture recoverable from the audit store on a deployment with no viz traffic. |
| `server.default_certs_generated` | Server | Emitted ONCE on the boot that generated the built-in default CA + leaves (the zero-config first-boot set). `principal=system`, `target_id=default-certs`, `detail` carries `ca_fingerprint=<SHA-256>`, `result=warning` (the install is running on built-in certificates). |
| `server.default_certs_in_use` | Server | Periodic (about every 300 s) reminder while the server is serving built-in default certificates. `principal=system`, `target_id=default-certs`, `result=warning`. |
| `server.default_certs_server_identity_upgraded` | Server | Emitted ONCE on the boot that re-issued an existing install's `default-server.pem` in place to add the server-only identity URI SAN `yuzu://<ca-fingerprint>/server` (#6016) — same root, same key, so existing gateway pins stay valid. `principal=system`, `target_id=default-certs`, `detail=Re-issued default-server.pem with the server identity URI SAN (same root, same key); superseded_serial=<hex> new_serial=<hex> superseded_revoked=<true\|false> ca_fingerprint=<SHA-256>`. `result=success` when the superseded serial is revoked in the CA inventory (reason `superseded`: bookkeeping, not a key compromise); `result=warning` with `superseded_revoked=false` when that revoke could not be confirmed — the old row may still be active (unless `replacement_withdrawn=true`, below) and should be revoked from the CA page or `POST /api/v1/ca/revoke` (it is not retried: the next boot finds the set intact). When an operator revoked the old serial for a real reason (for example key compromise) while the upgrade ran, the row is a `warning` with `superseded_revoked=false` and `replacement_withdrawn=true`: the new certificate over the same key was revoked too, so re-key the set (see Upgrading). If the detail carries `replacement_withdraw_failed=true` instead, revoking the new certificate failed or matched nothing (the error log names its serial): revoke it manually and re-key. Not emitted on a fresh install (the leaf is minted with the URI; see `server.default_certs_generated`) or when the upgrade was skipped. |
| `server.unsigned_packs_allowed` | ProductPack | Emitted ONCE at server startup when `--allow-unsigned-packs` / `YUZU_ALLOW_UNSIGNED_PACKS=1` is set (#802 / W7.4). `principal=system`, `target_id=signature_enforcement`, `detail` describes the flag source, `result=success`. Pairs with the `[SECURITY] product pack signature enforcement DISABLED by configuration` warn line in operator logs. SIEM rules can detect a server running with the relaxed posture even on deployments with no pack-install traffic. |
| `server.unsigned_definitions_allowed` | InstructionDefinition | Sibling of `server.unsigned_packs_allowed`, emitted ONCE at server startup when `--allow-unsigned-definitions` / `YUZU_ALLOW_UNSIGNED_DEFINITIONS=1` is set (#1073 / W7.4 sibling-gap closure). `principal=system`, `target_id=signature_enforcement`, `detail` describes the flag source, `result=success`. Pairs with the `InstructionStore: signature enforcement DISABLED by configuration` warn line in operator logs. Same SIEM use case: identifies servers running with the relaxed instruction-import posture. |
| `config.admin_group_set` | AuthConfig | Emitted ONCE per configured flag at server startup when `--oidc-admin-group` and/or `--saml-admin-group` is non-empty (#1829). `principal=system`, `target_id` is `oidc` or `saml` (one row per configured provider — up to two rows on a server with both wired), `detail=provider=<oidc\|saml>;admin_group=<value>` (the already-trimmed group value — a group identifier, not a secret), `result=success`. Makes the standing "which group grants admin via SSO" posture recoverable from the audit store even on a deployment with no SSO logins yet. |
| `mcp.session.*` | McpSession | MCP Streamable HTTP session lifecycle (track 2f PR 1/2/3b) — `initialize`, `GET`/`DELETE /mcp/v1/`, and the streamed-POST admission path all funnel through this one action family. No RBAC permission gate — this is a transport-level pre-auth/session check, not an authorized operation. `mcp.session.open`: a session was minted on a successful `initialize`; `target_id` is the new session id's first 8 characters, `detail` empty (the principal is derived from `req` by the audit layer, not stamped from the minted id — the session-id prefix in `target_id` is what correlates an `open` row to its later `close`/`reject` rows, governance CONS-N2). `mcp.session.close`, `result=success`: either an explicit `DELETE /mcp/v1/` (`target_id`=session-id prefix, `detail` empty), or the server force-terminating the session because a `GET` resume's `Last-Event-ID` cursor fell outside the replay window (`detail=reason=replay_window_exceeded cid=<cid>`, #2793) — **`result=success` here describes the close itself succeeding, not the triggering request**, which still answers `404` to the client. `mcp.session.reject`, `result=failure`: every denial across all three methods and both the pre-admission and streamed-POST-internal admission checks — `Origin` not allowed, unsupported `MCP-Protocol-Version`, a missing or presented-but-invalid `Mcp-Session-Id` (unknown, expired, or another principal's — one answer for all three, no cross-principal existence oracle, Decision 15(a)/CH-8), a concurrency cap — on `initialize` (`per_principal_cap`/`global_cap`/`id_generation`, `McpSessionRegistry`'s own session-mint tracking) or on `GET`'s stream attach (`per_principal_stream_cap`/`global_stream_cap`, the shared cross-surface `StreamBudget` — a distinct reason vocabulary from the mint-time cap despite the similar names), `GET`-specific causes (`not_acceptable`, `stream_handover_pending`, `terminal_poisoned`), and the streamed-POST admission denials (`--mcp-enable-streamed-post`, 2f PR 3b) — `post_per_principal_cap`/`post_global_cap`/`post_pin_slots`/`post_duplicate_request_id`/`post_unknown_session`, whose `detail` additionally carries `surface=post dispatched=false` since a streamed-POST denial happens before any execution row exists. `target_id` is empty on every cause that rejects before a session is read at all — `Origin`, protocol version, `missing_session_header` (there is no header to reflect), and the `initialize` mint-cap family (rejection happens before `initialize` even looks for one) — and carries the sanitized session-id prefix on every other cause: `unknown_session` (any method — the presented, not-yet-validated id), and, on `GET` specifically, `not_acceptable`/`stream_handover_pending`/`terminal_poisoned`/the stream-attach cap pair and the streamed-POST denials (all reached only once `validate_and_touch` has already confirmed a real, live session, so the id is never empty there). `detail=reason=<X> cid=<correlation_id>` for every cause. The full closed `reason=` set (and which values are `mcp.session.close` vs `mcp.session.reject`) is in `docs/observability-conventions.md`. Every *presented* session-id prefix reaching any of these three verbs is sanitized (`sanitize_detail_value`) at every call site that reads one from the `Mcp-Session-Id` header, since it is attacker-controlled until it validates — raw bytes could otherwise inject the `;`/`=` field separators a `k=v;k=v` `detail` string relies on (#2917 closed the one remaining unsanitized site, on `POST`'s presented-session check). `mcp.session.open`'s `target_id` is the exception: it is the server's own freshly-minted id, not attacker input, so it is stamped unsanitized. |
| `mcp.bridge.*` | Execution | Server-side lifecycle events of the MCP progress bridge (track 2f PR 3a), NOT operator actions - emitted from the background sweep/projector (and, for the three client-driven verbs `mcp.bridge.cancel`, `mcp.bridge.dispatch_failure` and `mcp.bridge.pin_displaced_for_admission`, from the request thread that lost, unwound, or was admitted), so `principal=system` for the bridge's own background work (sweep/projector) - **except** the three client-driven verbs, EVERY `mcp.bridge.cancel` row (whether the cancel detached a live streamed response or was consumed at `arm()` - as of the SSE-on-`POST` rung both carry the canceller; the session gate binds the record's principal to the authenticated caller, so the two cannot diverge) `mcp.bridge.dispatch_failure`, and `mcp.bridge.pin_displaced_for_admission` (#2740 — a streamed admission reclaimed a session slot from an already-committed final no wire took delivery of: either a parked record's, or an ORPHAN pin whose owning record a teardown erased without unpinning. `target_id` is the displaced final's `execution_id`, empty for an orphan because no record survives to name it, and the `detail` says which case it was), which carry the authenticated caller as `principal` so a cancel is attributable to the client that asked for it (Decision 15(j) non-repudiation) - and `target_id` is the `execution_id` - **except** in two cases where `target_id` is empty: a record reaped by the arming reaper before `subscribe()` assigned an execution id, and a `pin_displaced_for_admission` row for an ORPHAN pin, whose owning record no longer exists to name one — that row carries `released ring event_id=<n>` in its `detail` instead, which is per-session and so must be read together with the row's own session context (that row is therefore not joinable to an execution; the `detail` still identifies the disposition). `result` is normally `success`, but every verb that routes through the claimed-teardown path (all of them except `mcp.bridge.cancel`, `mcp.bridge.dispatch_failure` and `mcp.bridge.pin_displaced_for_admission`, which are transitions rather than teardowns) emits **`result=failure`** when the teardown could not settle one of the three resources it owns (`detail` begins `teardown incomplete: ...` and names what is retained - see `yuzu_mcp_bridge_teardown_incomplete_total{reason}` and `docs/ops-runbooks/mcp-bridge-teardown-recovery.md`), or when the terminal-frame publish ladder poisoned the session, threw, or was never reached (`detail` states which, and never asserts a delivery or a poisoning that did not happen - #2506). **Upgrade note:** before v0.13.0 these rows were unconditionally stamped `result=success` regardless of outcome; a SIEM rule that assumed the constant needs updating. The verb family: `mcp.bridge.done_reap` (a settled GET-only record was reaped), `mcp.bridge.session_dead` (a record torn down because its session idle-expired), `mcp.bridge.arming_reaped` (an orphaned reserved-but-never-armed record was reclaimed by the age reaper), `mcp.bridge.cancel` (a cancel took effect - either consumed at arm, or detaching a LIVE streamed response), `mcp.bridge.dispatch_failure` (a request failed AFTER its work was dispatched, so its record was PARKED rather than abandoned - the execution keeps running and its result stays fetchable by `execution_id`; unlike the pre-dispatch failure paths this deliberately does NOT cancel the execution), `mcp.bridge.pin_acked` and `mcp.bridge.forced_expire` (parked-result teardown), and `mcp.bridge.pin_displaced_for_admission` (#2740 — a streamed admission reclaimed a session slot; carries the admitting principal). **Correlate the last two:** since #2740 a released pin is no longer proof a client consumed anything, so a `pin_acked` row that follows a `pin_displaced_for_admission` row for the same execution records a reclaim, NOT an ack — the action name predates the reclaim and is kept for taxonomy stability. `mcp.bridge.shutdown_reap` (#2517) is the one AGGREGATE exception to the per-execution shape above: emitted at most once per process shutdown, with `target_id`/`execution_id` empty and `detail` naming the COUNT of records reclaimed (`"poisoned N claimed-but-unpublished record(s) at shutdown..."`) — a claimed-but-unresolved teardown `shutdown()`'s own walk found abandoned by a raced sweep, rather than a per-record row (which would re-enter the teardown's exactly-once arbitration). Always `result=success`, `principal=system`. No metric backs it (the process is exiting before any scrape); a shutdown-time reclaim that WAS already resolved gets no row at all (the ordinary silent-reclaim case, `docs/observability-conventions.md`'s comp-S1 bullet). `mcp.bridge.teardown_retry` (#2513) is the `audit_action` a LATER sweep's retry pass uses when it re-enters a teardown a prior attempt left incomplete - never the original claim verb (`session_dead`, `pin_acked`, etc.), which already carries its own row for the first attempt. `result` on the attempt that finally settles the record (`detail` says `"recovered on a retry attempt"` - a static literal, not the attempt count, since `teardown_claimed` is `noexcept` end to end and every string on it must be allocation-free outside a contained boundary) is NOT unconditionally `success` - it mirrors the terminal's fate exactly as it would on a first attempt, per the general rule two sentences up: `success` only when the terminal was delivered (published, or none was owed), `failure` when it was not, independent of whether the retention strand itself resolved. A retry can therefore settle the record (mechanically recovering the subscription/charge/erase, and counting `outcome="recovered"`) while replaying a terminal that a PRIOR attempt already poisoned or never built - that row still carries `result=failure` with the same `"recovered on a retry attempt"` detail, since the retention strand and the terminal's fate are independent facts. `result=failure` on a retry whose retention strand fails again, same detail shape (mechanical failure plus a `"(retry-eligible on a later sweep)"` or `"(retry budget exhausted; retained until shutdown)"` suffix, chosen from two static literals per bail site, never built at runtime) as the first attempt's row, until `Config::teardown_retry_max` is reached, at which point the exhausted variant fires and no further retries are attempted for that record (see `yuzu_mcp_bridge_teardown_retry_total{outcome}`). All of these are LIVE as of the SSE-on-`POST` rung (2f PR 3b), which wired their callers. The operator action that *starts* progress streaming (`execute_instruction`) is separately audited under its own `mcp.execute_instruction` verb with the real principal - the bridge does not replace it. |
| `mcp.summarize_working_set` | mcp_tool | The MCP tool `summarize_working_set` was called (#3526, #4753). `target_id=summarize_working_set`. `result=success` carries `detail=<kind>:<id>` (the caller-supplied id is neutralised for `k=v` and CR/LF forgery and capped at 128 bytes, for every kind). The scope-collapse `result=denied` row is written only for a CONFINED caller (management-group scoped) on `kind=execution` who names an execution id that is absent or outside the caller's fleet-read scope; `detail=not found or outside caller's fleet-read scope: <id>` (id neutralised and capped at 128 bytes) and no `success` row is written for that call. Other `denied` rows on this action (tier refusal, permission-gate refusal, service-scoped default-deny) are separate and can occur regardless of RBAC state. The `id` argument is advertised with `maxLength` 128, and a NUL byte in a `kind=execution` id is rejected with an invalid-params error before any lookup. The `denied` row cannot tell a typo from an out-of-scope probe, by design (the tool's narrative for such an id reads exactly like an absent one, so existence is not an oracle). An UNCONFINED caller's absent id stays `result=success`. The scope-collapse `denied` row is therefore only emitted for a confined caller, so it is never written with RBAC off. Permission gates: `Infrastructure:Read` first, then `Execution:Read` through the fleet-read gate for `kind=execution`. |
| `mcp.list_hardware_ci` | mcp_tool | The MCP tool `list_hardware_ci` was called (twin of `GET /api/v1/hardware`). `target_id=list_hardware_ci`. Gate `Inventory:Read` through the fleet-read gate (a gate-level denial is audited under `auth.fleet_read_required`, not here). Written by every call except one the fleet-read gate refused. `result=success` carries the same `detail` as the paired `inventory.devices` row (`hardware list (MCP): devices=<n> omitted=<m>`, or `devices=<n> scope=authority_scoped` for a confined caller) and is written together with it; the tool **fails closed**, so if either row cannot be written the data is withheld (see `inventory.devices`). `result=failure` carries `hardware roster read failed` or `hardware provider unwired`, each paired with a failure row on `inventory.devices` when the domain audit sink is wired. A refused argument (a wrongly typed or out-of-range `q`, `os`, `status`, `sort`, `dir`, `tag`, `offset` or `limit`, an enum value that is not exactly lowercase, or a `tag` that is not a valid tag key) writes ONLY this generic row, `result=denied`, `detail=invalid arguments: <reason>` (or `invalid arguments: tag key`); no `inventory.devices` row is written for a refusal. A non-object `arguments` value is refused earlier by the registry's central check (`result=denied`, `detail=arguments must be a JSON object correlation_id=<cid>`). |
| `mcp.get_hardware_ci` | mcp_tool | The MCP tool `get_hardware_ci` was called (twin of `GET /api/v1/hardware/{id}`). `target_id=get_hardware_ci`; the requested device is in the paired `inventory.device.ci` row's `target_id`, not here. Gate `Inventory:Read` through the fleet-read gate, then a per-id scope check. `result=success` or `result=failure` carries the same `detail` as the paired `inventory.device.ci` row (`hardware CI record (MCP) state=<...>`) and is written together with it; the tool **fails closed** (see `inventory.device.ci`). Three refusals write ONLY this generic row: `result=denied` `invalid arguments: agent_id` (missing, not a string, empty, over 256 bytes or containing a byte below 0x20); `result=denied` `device out of the caller's scope`; and `result=failure` `not found`. The caller receives the identical `-32602` `not found` for the last two (no existence oracle). For a confined caller the two cases are also indistinguishable in this log, because a nonexistent id is outside the caller's visible set and takes the out-of-scope branch; a confined caller reaches the `not found` row only for an id inside its own visible set, so neither row implies that the id exists. The out-of-scope and not-found details carry the requested id as ` id=<agent_id>` (passed through the audit token filter), which is written only after the id has passed the audit-safe check. |
| `mcp.list_quarantined_devices` | mcp_tool | The MCP tool `list_quarantined_devices` was called (twin of `GET /api/v1/quarantine`). `target_id=list_quarantined_devices`. Gate `Security:Read` through the fleet-read gate (a gate-level denial is audited under `auth.fleet_read_required`). **This is the only audit row for the read**: the REST `GET /api/v1/quarantine` writes no row at all, and the tool has no domain verb. It is **set-and-proceed**: `result=success` carries `limit=<n> returned=<n> more=true\|false`, and if the row cannot be persisted the data is still returned with `audit_persisted:false` in the result. `result=failure` carries `quarantine list unavailable; store degraded` (the tool answers `-32603` with `retry_after_ms` and the envelope carries `audit_persisted:false` if this row was dropped) or `dependency unwired; failing closed`. A `limit` argument that is not a JSON integer from 1 to 500 is refused with `-32602` and writes `result=denied` `invalid arguments: limit`; a non-object `arguments` value is refused by the registry's central check (`result=denied`, `detail=arguments must be a JSON object correlation_id=<cid>`). |
| `approval.read` | Approval | An approval ticket was read through the REST approval routes: a single fetch (`detail` `status=<status>`), the list (`surface=list count=<n>`) or the pending count (`surface=count count=<n>`). `result=success`; set-and-proceed, so a row that cannot be stored does not stop the read |
| `bundle.dispatch` | Execution | A bundle was dispatched by `POST /api/v1/bundles`. `target_id` is the correlation id and `detail` is `agent=<id> steps=<n>` on `result=success`; `result=failure` (and a `503`) when the dispatch threw |
| `bundle.collate` | Execution | A bundle result was collated by `GET /api/v1/bundles/{id}`. `result` is `success` (`detail` `complete=0` or `1`), `denied` (not found or not owned) or `failure` (response store degraded) |
| `compliance.agent_statuses.view` | Policy | The per-device statuses of one compliance policy were read, by the REST read or the dashboard fragment. `target_id` is the policy id; `result=success`; `detail` carries `agents=<n>`. A row that cannot be stored fails the REST read with `503` and no device data |
| `tar.retention_paused.view` | Infrastructure | A service-scoped API token was refused on the fleet-wide TAR retention-paused read. `result=denied` only; a successful read writes no row |
| `device_token.create` / `device_token.revoke` | DeviceToken | A device token was created or revoked. `result` is `success` or `failure` (`detail` names the cause). A revoke of an unknown id is not audited. The routes exist only when a device token store is wired, which this build does not do |
| `api.v1.events.subscribe` | Execution | A subscription to `GET /api/v1/events` for one execution. `result=success` (`detail` `correlation_id=<id>`), or `denied` when the execution does not exist or is outside the caller's fleet-read scope |
| `license.activate` / `license.remove` | License | A license was activated (`target_id` is the new license id, `detail` the organization) or removed. `result=success`; set-and-proceed. The routes exist only when a license store is wired, which this build does not do |
| `management_group.update` | ManagementGroup | A management group was updated by `PUT /api/v1/management-groups/{id}`. `target_id` is the group id and `detail` the group name; `result=success` |
| `plugin_config.set` / `plugin_config.delete` | PluginConfig | A plugin config value was upserted or deleted. Written twice: `result=attempted` BEFORE the change (if it cannot be stored the request is refused with `503` and nothing changes), then `success` or `failure`. `detail` for a set records only the length of the value |
| `plugin_secret.set` / `plugin_secret.delete` | PluginSecret | A plugin secret was sealed or deleted. Same attempted-then-outcome pattern as `plugin_config.set`. The value is never recorded |
| `preflight.run.view` | Scope | A service-scoped API token was refused on the pre-flight runs list. `result=denied` only |
| `result_set.create` | ResultSet | A result set create or re-evaluate was attempted. `result` is `success`, `denied` or `failure` and `detail` carries a reason token. The success row is written after the set exists |
| `result_set.pin` / `result_set.unpin` / `result_set.delete` | ResultSet | A result set was pinned, unpinned or deleted. `result=success`; a pin refused at the pin limit is `denied` and a failed pin or unpin is `failure` |
| `result_set.access` | ResultSet | An id was not found or not owned by the caller. `result=denied`, `detail` `not found or not owned` |
| `result_set.list` / `result_set.members` / `result_set.lineage` | ResultSet | The store read behind the list, member or lineage route failed. `result=failure` only; successful reads are not audited |
| `result_set.heal` | ResultSet | A re-evaluate found a stored query that nests too deeply and discarded it. `result` is `success` if it was healed, else `failure` |
| `result_set.create.access_denied` and the other `result_set.<op>.access_denied` verbs (`list`, `detail`, `members`, `lineage`, `pin`, `unpin`, `delete`) | ResultSet | A service-scoped API token was refused on that result set route. `result=denied` |
| `scope.preview.access_denied` | ResultSet | A service-scoped API token called `POST /api/v1/scope/preview` and was refused. `result=denied` |
| `sle.agent.decommission` | Agent | A durable-erasure decommission of one agent. Written first with `result=attempt` (if it cannot be stored the erase is refused with `503` and `Sec-Audit-Failed`), then `success` or `partial` with the erase counts |
| `ca.cert.issued` | CodeSigningCertificate | A code-signing certificate was issued (`result=success`, `target_id` the serial) or refused (`denied` for a weak key or a bad validity, otherwise `failure`; `target_id` the requested label) |
| `ca.root_csr.exported` | CaRoot | The CA signing request was exported. `result=success`, or `failure` when generating it failed |
| `kek.rotate` / `kek.rewrap` | Secret | The secrets key-encryption key was rotated or the stored secrets were re-wrapped. `target_id=kek`; `result` is `success` (`detail` `new_version=<n>` or `rows_rewrapped=<n>`) or `failure` |
| `settings.tls.read` / `settings.https.read` / `settings.analytics.read` / `settings.plugin_signing.read` | TlsConfig / TlsConfig / AnalyticsConfig / PluginSigning | One of the REST settings reads (`/settings/tls`, `/settings/https`, `/settings/analytics`, or the v2 plugin signing policy call). `result=success`. A row that cannot be stored fails the read with `503` and no data |
| `software_package.create` | SoftwarePackage | A software package was created (`result=success`) or a package field was rejected (`denied`). The routes exist only when a software deployment store is wired, which this build does not do |
| `software_deployment.create` / `software_deployment.start` / `software_deployment.rollback` / `software_deployment.cancel` | SoftwareDeployment | A software deployment was created, started, rolled back or cancelled. `result=success`. Same availability note as `software_package.create` |
| `upload_grant.mint` | UploadGrant | An upload grant was minted. `result=success` (`target_id` is the grant id, `detail` `agent_id=<id>`) or `failure` |
| `upload_grant.session_open` / `upload_grant.chunk` / `upload_grant.commit` / `upload_grant.cancel` | UploadGrant | The device side of an upload. `session_open` is written for a success or a refused redemption; `chunk` only for a failure (`expired`, `size_exceeded`); `commit` for a success or a failure (`expired`, `hash_mismatch`); `cancel` for a success or an expired session. `target_id` is the upload id, or the grant id for a redemption |
| `auth.admin_required` | (empty) or `endpoint` | A token or non-admin caller was refused on an admin-only route. `result=denied`; the full detail strings are in the authentication manual |

**Reconciling approval decisions.** After an audit-store fault, list the approval tickets that have more `attempted` rows than outcome rows, then check each one's real status (the Approvals page, or `GET /api/v1/approvals/{id}`). Compare counts per `target_id`; do not test whether an outcome row merely exists. Timestamps are epoch seconds; set the window to the incident and leave out the last minute, where a request may be in flight. Start the window before the first decision on the tickets you care about, and re-run a suspect ticket over its whole life: a window that starts inside a ticket's life can cut an early `attempted` row while keeping a later outcome row, and that surplus outcome row can offset one lost `success` row. Row timestamps are the writing replica's clock, while the window is measured on the database `now()`, so widen the window by the observed skew when a clock anomaly is suspected. A returned ticket is suspect, not proven: an outcome row was lost, or the server stopped between the `attempted` row and the decision. The same query, with the surrounding procedure, is in `docs/ops-runbooks/audit-store-clock-guard.md` (`YuzuAuditPersistFailures`).

```sql
SELECT target_id,
       count(*) FILTER (WHERE result = 'attempted') AS attempted_rows,
       count(*) FILTER (WHERE result = 'success'
                           OR (result = 'denied' AND detail NOT LIKE 'approver_assurance:%'))
                                                    AS outcome_rows
FROM audit_store.audit_events
WHERE action IN ('approval.approve', 'approval.reject')
  AND timestamp >= extract(epoch from now() - interval '24 hours')::bigint
  AND timestamp <  extract(epoch from now() - interval '1 minute')::bigint
GROUP BY target_id
HAVING count(*) FILTER (WHERE result = 'attempted')
     > count(*) FILTER (WHERE result = 'success'
                           OR (result = 'denied' AND detail NOT LIKE 'approver_assurance:%'))
ORDER BY target_id;
```

**Result vocabulary.** `result` is predominantly `success` or `denied`, with `failure` for a failure the handler audits itself. `partial` means the change committed but could not be fully delivered or applied (`api_token.rotate`, `engine_principal.credential.rotate`, `engine_principal.credential.mint`, and others named in their rows); it is never `failure`, because the change exists. `attempted` is written FIRST by operations that fail closed on audit (among them the bell, the approvals approve/reject decision, the MFA clears (`mfa.disabled` from Settings and `mfa.reset.breakglass` from the `--mfa-reset` command: each writes an `attempted` intent row before the factor is touched, so every clear leaves two rows and a `blocked:` self-target refusal leaves one `error` row with no `attempted` row), the plugin-config writes, the instruction-set and dashboard dispatch writes, the Guardian policy-generation raise (`guaranteed_state.policy_generation.raise`), the DEX score policy writes (`dex.score_policy.update`, `dex.score_policy.reset`), and the Integrations writes: `webhook.*`, `offload_target.*`, `directory.sync`, `directory.group_mapping.update`, `settings.mcp`): an outcome row follows it (a rejected request leaves `attempted` then `denied` or `failure`), and an `attempted` row with no outcome row means "started, outcome unknown". A "failures only" filter of `result != "success"` therefore also matches every `attempted` row; exclude it, or key on the outcome row. `no_change` is an outcome that is not a failure: a `dex.score_policy.reset` of a policy that was never written records `attempted` then `no_change`, and the same filter matches it too. On the Integrations writes, a REST `2xx` that carries `Sec-Audit-Failed: true` means the OUTCOME row was lost after the change was made: the `attempted` row is then the only record that it happened. The set is not closed in practice - `ok`, `error`, `warning` and `skipped` also appear on specific verbs. `denied` is the usual token for an authorization or validation rejection with a real principal behind it, but that is a convention rather than an invariant: `enrollment.token_consumed` and `engine_principal.credential.rotate` both use `failure` for what are really rejections, and `instruction.scope_resolution_failed` was emitting a self-audited `failure` before the `mcp.bridge.*` family existed. Unlike those still-standing violators, the `mcp.<tool_name>` unknown-tool row was corrected to `denied` in #2445 — it used to audit `failure` for a client-caused rejection (the caller named a tool that doesn't exist), inconsistent with most other denials on the `tools/call` surface. That surface still has several of its own violators, unchanged by #2445 and not fully enumerated here (tracked for a full sweep in #3176): `set_tag`'s validation-failure rejection, `execute_bundle`'s out-of-scope rejection, and the access-review campaign family's not-found rejections are all client-caused but audit `failure`. `delete_tag`'s not-found rejection is client-caused too but does NOT belong on this list — it audits a distinct third token, `not_found`, not `failure`. **So a SIEM rule that surfaces failure branches by filtering on `denied` alone will miss several families, including every background-actor row.** Filter on `result != "success"` when you want all of them. Note `GET /api/v1/audit` does not currently expose a `result` query parameter, so that filter has to be applied by the SIEM after export rather than server-side. `denied` is used for RBAC rejections and for every 400/404/409 branch where the handler explicitly audits the failure — including Guardian `rule.create` (400 missing fields, 409 conflict), `rule.update` (400 invalid body, 404 not found, 409 conflict), `rule.delete` (404 not found), `push` (400 non-object body), and the Phase 8.3 `offload_target.create` (400 validation_failed) / `offload_target.delete` (404 not_found) handlers. SIEM rules that filter on `result == "success"` will match every completed mutation including `guaranteed_state.push` (which returns 202 rather than 201/200 because agent fan-out is asynchronous). To surface probe/fuzz traffic on the REST surface, filter on `result == "denied"` scoped to the actions you care about — every mutating branch produces a row.

The `/auto` pre-flight verbs add two non-denial outcomes: `no_devices` (a `preflight.run` whose scope resolved to no visible devices — an operator no-op, not a rejection) and `noop` (a `preflight.run.delete` whose run id was unknown or not owner-visible — a no-op rather than a permission denial, which keeps a non-owner from distinguishing "exists" from "doesn't exist"). Neither is a `denied`; SIEM rules counting denials should not include them.

**Behavioural-PII access-audit rows: `result=success` means "authorised and attempted," not always "disclosed" (#4858).** The per-device / per-signal PII routes (`dex.device.view`, `dex.signal.view`, `guardian.device.view`, `dex.app_perf.devices.view`, and their MCP twins) split across postures **by (verb, surface), not by verb alone** — a SIEM/auditor reading raw rows must know which applies to a given row before drawing a conclusion from `result` alone:

- **Pre-read (most dashboard fragments and REST gates, e.g. `dex.device.view`, `dex.signal.view`, the MCP tool `get_dex_device_app_perf`'s `dex.device.app_perf.view` row, and the MCP tool `list_guardian_events`'s `dex.device.view` row).** The audit row is written BEFORE the underlying store read runs, with a literal `result=success` asserting only that the request was authorised and the read was ATTEMPTED. If the store then degrades (see `yuzu_server_guardian_read_degrade_total` / `yuzu_server_app_perf_read_degrade_total` in `docs/user-manual/metrics.md`), the dashboard renders a placeholder or the REST/MCP caller gets a `503`/retryable error — on top of an audit row that already recorded `success`. Do not read `success` on these verbs as proof any PII was actually disclosed; corroborate against the read-degrade metric for the same time window if that matters to your investigation.
- **Post-read by outcome (e.g. `dex.app_perf.devices.view` on every surface, and `guardian.device.view` on REST **and** the MCP tool).** The audit row is written AFTER the read, with `result` set to `success`/`failure` by the read's actual outcome — here `success` does mean the read itself succeeded (though a genuinely-empty result, e.g. an unrecognised `agent_id`, is still `success` — a legitimate empty answer, not a failure). **`guardian.device.view` is surface-dependent**: on REST and MCP it is this post-read-by-outcome posture, but the device-page Guardian lens dashboard fragment (`GET /fragments/device/guardian`) emits the SAME verb pre-read with a constant `result=success` instead (the first bullet's shape) — the verb name alone does not tell you which posture produced a given row; check which surface emitted it.
- **Post-read constant success (the MCP `get_dex_device_score` tool's `dex.device.view` row, and the MCP `get_dex_device_history` tool's `dex.device.view` row).** The row is written AFTER the read but always records `result=success` regardless of the read's `degraded` outcome (the MCP set-and-proceed convention for the AUDIT row — there is no response-header channel). This is distinct from `audit_persisted`, a SEPARATE signal for a SEPARATE failure mode: `audit_persisted:false` appears in a *successful* tool response body only when the audit write itself failed to persist (the row never made it to storage); it is **not present** when the underlying read degraded instead (that case returns an `a4_error` response — `get_dex_device_score`/`get_dex_device_app_perf` both — with no `audit_persisted` field at all, since there is no success body to carry it in). Do not conflate "the audit row didn't persist" (`audit_persisted:false`) with "the read degraded" (an error response, no such field) — corroborate a degraded MCP read against the store's own read-degrade metric instead.

The Hardware CI-record verbs `inventory.device.ci` and `inventory.device.software` add one case to the post-read-by-outcome group: when the device roster read failed, the row carries `result=failure` even though the record itself is still served (the device identity is shown as unavailable); key on the `identity=roster unreadable` detail token, not on `result` alone. The not-served arms (nothing else shown, by the two predicates in the `inventory.device.ci` row: the REST record's `503` and the dashboard fragment's "Device roster unreadable" page) carry `state=roster unreadable` with the same `result=failure`, so a SIEM rule should key on both tokens. The MCP tool `get_hardware_ci` writes the same `state=roster unreadable` token (with `(MCP)` in the detail prefix) and the same `result=failure` on its `inventory.device.ci` row, and `list_hardware_ci` writes `result=failure` `hardware roster read failed` on `inventory.devices`; each also writes a matching `result=failure` generic `mcp.<tool>` row. `get_hardware_ci` additionally writes `state=software or tags unreadable` (also `result=failure`) when the device is otherwise absent and only the software or tag read failed, so a SIEM rule should key on all of these tokens.

This split is a known, accepted state across surfaces, not a defect in any one of them; converging on one posture is deferred to the WS-B2 audit-relocation step (ADR-0031). See `server/core/src/rest_audit.hpp`'s file banner for the authoritative, code-level statement of this contract.

The `/auto` deploy verbs share this pattern: `deployment.create` emits `resumed` (re-attached to an in-flight deployment for the same run instead of creating a duplicate) and `no_devices` (empty go-cohort after scope re-intersection); `deployment.delete` emits `noop` (unknown / not-owner-visible id). None is a `denied`. `deployment.advance` is high-volume (one row per poll tick); SIEM rules counting operator-initiated actions should treat a same-`deployment_id` burst as one session, not many.

**gRPC audit-failure signal.** REST surfaces set the `Sec-Audit-Failed: true` response header (and an `audit_emitted=false` body field) when an audit row fails to persist. The gRPC Register/Subscribe paths carry the equivalent as trailing metadata **`x-yuzu-audit-failed: true`** (#1063; the key is a shared constant in `grpc_audit_signal.hpp` so it cannot drift between the direct-agent and gateway-proxy services). In both transports the underlying operation still completes — enrollment proceeds, a peer-mismatch Subscribe is still rejected — only the SOC 2 evidence chain is degraded for that one request. SRE alerting should cover both signals; SIEM pipelines ingesting gRPC telemetry should watch for the `x-yuzu-audit-failed` trailer on Register/Subscribe RPCs. The gateway-upstream peer-authorization guard deliberately does **not** set this trailer on its refusals (see `session.gateway_peer_denied` above): a refused call is decided before its audit row is written and its counter is complete either way.

## REST API

**Who may read the audit trail.** Reading it needs the `AuditLog:Read`
permission, on every surface: the REST routes below, the dashboard viewer, and
the MCP `query_audit_log` / `audit_engine_no_admin` tools and
`yuzu://audit/recent` resource.

- **RBAC disabled (the shipped default):** only an `admin`-role session may
  read the trail (#5771). Any other authenticated session gets `403`, audited with a `detail` beginning
  `topology floor: non-admin role denied AuditLog:Read` (match on the prefix: an
  MCP-tier token's row appends `(mcp_tier=<tier>)`). Engine principals and
  service-scoped tokens are refused as before.
- **RBAC enabled:** `AuditLog:Read` is an ordinary grant. The seeded
  `Administrator`, `PlatformEngineer`, `Operator`, `ITServiceOwner` and `Viewer`
  roles hold it; a custom role can be granted it. A holder sees every row.

See [RBAC](rbac.md#the-authorization-topology-floor-2376) for the floor and for
how to grant a non-admin the read.

### GET /api/v1/audit

Query audit events with filtering and pagination. Maximum page size is 1000.

**Query parameters:**

| Parameter | Type | Description |
|---|---|---|
| `limit` | integer | Number of events to return (default 100, max 1000) |
| `principal` | string | Filter by principal username |
| `action` | string | Filter by action (exact match) |
| `target_type` | string | Filter by target type (exact match) |
| `target_id` | string | Filter by target id (exact match); requires `target_type` |
| `since` | integer | Inclusive lower bound, epoch seconds. Omit for no bound; `0` is accepted and matches the same rows as omitting it. Negative is `400` |
| `until` | integer | Inclusive upper bound, epoch seconds. Omit for no bound; `0` or negative is `400` |
| `offset` | integer | Rows to skip, `0`..`10000` (default 0) |

A negative `since`, and an `until` of `0` or below, are rejected (`400` on `/api/audit` and `GET /api/v1/audit`, `-32602` on MCP `query_audit_log`; `to=0` is `400` on `GET /api/v1/audit/auth-sample`): the bounds are presence-tracked, and a literal `-3600` or `until=0` used to mean no bound and returned the whole log. Omit the parameter for no bound; `since=0` (`from=0` on the auth sample) is accepted and matches the same rows as omitting it. A strict-parse `400` on these audit and analytics reads (`GET /api/audit`, `GET /api/v1/audit`, `GET /api/v1/audit/auth-sample`, `GET /api/analytics/recent`) writes no audit row, and neither does one on the notification and patch lists, the legacy policy lists, the pre-flight run form, the OTA rollout change, the DEX blast-radius thresholds, the API-token form, or the DEX signal, DEX perf and network device lists, because each is answered before any audit write. These routes audit their rejection instead, with a `result=denied` row for a malformed or out-of-range number (see their rows): `POST /api/settings/enrollment-tokens`, `POST /api/settings/enrollment-tokens/batch`, `POST /api/v1/offload-targets` and `POST /api/v1/guaranteed-state/rules` (an out-of-range `version`). `GET /api/v1/guaranteed-state/events` parses `limit` and `offset` before it writes its `dex.device.view` row, so its `400` leaves no access row.

**Example --- fetch the 20 most recent events:**

```bash
curl -s -b cookies.txt \
  'http://localhost:8080/api/v1/audit?limit=20' | jq .
```

**Response:**

```json
{
  "data": [
    {
      "id": 4101,
      "timestamp": 1710849600,
      "principal": "admin",
      "action": "tag.set",
      "result": "success",
      "target_type": "Tag",
      "target_id": "agent-001:environment",
      "detail": "Production"
    }
  ],
  "pagination": {
    "total": 20,
    "start": 0,
    "page_size": 100
  },
  "meta": {
    "api_version": "v1"
  }
}
```

**`total` is the number of rows returned in this response, not the number of
rows that matched.** `start` echoes `offset` and `page_size` echoes `limit`; a
page shorter than `limit` is the last page.
The MCP `query_audit_log` tool also returns `has_more`, true when more matching rows exist
past the page. Rows are ordered newest first and
rows with an identical timestamp break by `id` (newest first). On an unchanging
result set `offset` paging neither repeats nor skips rows; the log is live, so a
new event or a retention delete between page fetches shifts later pages. Pin the
window with a fixed `until` in the past and use `id` to de-duplicate. `offset` is
capped at 10000 and never clamped (a larger value is a `400`). To read deeper,
set `until` to the oldest `timestamp` already seen (inclusive), restart at
`offset=0`, and de-duplicate on `id`.

**Example --- filter by principal and action:**

```bash
curl -s -b cookies.txt \
  'http://localhost:8080/api/v1/audit?principal=admin&action=auth.login&limit=50' | jq .
```

**Example --- find all failed login attempts:**

```bash
curl -s -b cookies.txt \
  'http://localhost:8080/api/v1/audit?action=auth.login_failed' | jq .
```

### GET /api/audit (legacy)

The legacy endpoint caps `limit` at 1000 and rejects `offset` above 10000, the
same ceilings as `/api/v1/audit`. It answers `400` for a negative `since`, an `until` of `0` or below, an
inverted window, and a string filter holding a NUL or invalid UTF-8 (the v1 route does the
same for the string filters). New integrations should prefer `/api/v1/audit`, which also
rejects a `target_id` given without `target_type`.

**Query parameters:**

| Parameter | Type | Description |
|---|---|---|
| `limit` | integer | Number of events to return (default 100, max 1000) |
| `offset` | integer | Offset for pagination, `0`..`10000` (default 0; larger is a `400`) |
| `principal` | string | Filter by principal username |
| `action` | string | Filter by action (exact match) |
| `target_type` | string | Filter by target type |
| `target_id` | string | Filter by target identifier |
| `since` | integer | Only events after this Unix timestamp |
| `until` | integer | Only events before this Unix timestamp |

The legacy response envelope differs from v1:

```json
{
  "events": [ ... ],
  "count": 20,
  "total": 150
}
```

The legacy response includes additional fields per event not present in the v1
response: `principal_role`, `source_ip` (`id` is on both).

```bash
curl -s -b cookies.txt \
  'http://localhost:8080/api/audit?limit=1000&principal=admin' | jq .
```

## Dashboard viewer

The audit trail is readable from the dashboard at **Access > Audit and reviews**
(`/access/audit`). The page is open to every signed-in user, but each panel is gated by its own
permission and a panel your account cannot read says so instead of rendering empty.

**Audit log.** Needs `AuditLog:Read` (admin-only while RBAC is disabled). It reads the same
store, with the same gate, as `GET /api/v1/audit` and offers a subset of its filters (below), and shows only the
fields that route returns: time, principal, action, target, result and `detail`, plus the
event id in the row detail. The source address and the principal's role, which the legacy
`GET /api/audit` route also returns, are not shown. Filters:

- a time range of the last hour, 24 hours or 7 days (the page sets `since`; `until` is open);
- Principal and Action, both **exact matches**, not searches.

Rows are newest first, 50 to a page, and the page does not show a filtered total. Paging
stops at row 10,000 (the same `offset` ceiling as the REST routes); the page says so and asks
you to narrow the time range, it never silently shows a different page than the one asked
for. The log is live, so an event written while you page can shift later pages. If the audit
store does not answer, the panel is a red box, never an empty table: an unreadable log is not
the same thing as no activity. Reading the log writes no audit row, exactly like the REST
route.

**Authentication sample.** A random sample of sign-in, MFA, session and engine-principal
events for evidence, drawn only when you press **Draw sample** (never on load). It is
`GET /api/v1/audit/auth-sample` with the window left open; the sample size is 1 to 1000. The
panel states how many candidates the sample was drawn from and whether the pool was capped to
the newest events. Each draw is itself recorded as `audit.auth_sample.exported`; if that row
cannot be written the panel says so.

**Access reviews.** The same page lists review campaigns, shows a campaign's frozen grants
(undecided first) with its summary, and shows the current grant population on request. It needs
`AccessReview:Read` (the Administrator and Reviewer roles; admin-only while RBAC is disabled), a
different permission from the audit log: holding `AuditLog:Read` does not grant it. A caller who
also holds `AccessReview:Attest` can open a review, attest or flag a grant (with an optional
justification) and close a campaign; each is a call to the existing `/api/v1/access-reviews`
routes. **Flag for revocation records evidence only; it never revokes the grant.** Closing is
one-way and may leave grants undecided. `/access/audit?campaign=<id>` opens a campaign directly.
Viewing writes the same rows as the REST reads: `access_review.list` for the list,
`access_review.get` for a campaign and `access_review.exported` (`format=view`) for the current
grants, so opening a campaign leaves a row in the trail. If the access review store does not
answer, the panel is a red box, never "no reviews". The campaign list loads on every page view,
so a refused browser session writes no denied-access row for it; opening a campaign (including
by the `?campaign=<id>` link) and reading the current grants are deliberate requests, and a
refused caller writes the ordinary permission-denied row for each. A refused API or MCP token
writes one on every fragment, and an engine-class session is refused on all three with an
`access_review.*` row, `result=denied`, `detail=engine_session_denied`. Settings keeps an older
Access Reviews panel; viewing it writes no `access_review.*` row, so use this page when the
reading itself must be on the record.

Not available yet on this page: a campaign due date (campaigns have no due-date field) and a
button that exports a campaign's decisions (the **Export decisions** button is disabled). A
campaign's complete decisions are in the JSON from `GET /api/v1/access-reviews/{id}`; the
current grants download as CSV from the **Current grants** panel.

**Retention.** Shows the configured retention period (`--audit-retention-days`, 365 days by
default) and that cleanup runs hourly. It needs `ServerConfig:Read`, which is
Administrator-only, so it is fetched on request rather than on every page view. It is read only.

## Retention and cleanup

Audit events are retained for 365 days by default. A background thread runs
every hour and deletes events whose `ttl_expires_at` has passed. That TTL is
stamped once, when the row is written, as `insert time + retention window` -- it
is not derived from the event's own `timestamp`, and nothing ever rewrites it.
Deletion is permanent --- there is no soft-delete or archive step.

To preserve audit data beyond the retention window, export events periodically
using the REST API or forward them to an external system (see Planned Features
below).

### The retention clock guard

**Which alert sent you here?** Find it in the table below — they report different
conditions and the triage differs. The current rule set is
`docs/prometheus/yuzu-alerts.yml`.

| Alert | What it means | Go to |
|---|---|---|
| `YuzuServerRestartLoop` | The server has restarted more than 3 times in the last 3 hours --- a process-health signal, not itself an audit-store fault, but a common CAUSE of the retention alerts below. | [`ops-runbooks/audit-store-clock-guard.md`](../ops-runbooks/audit-store-clock-guard.md#yuzuserverrestartloop). |
| `YuzuAuditRetentionClockAnomaly` | A pass declined. Nothing was deleted. | Read on. |
| `YuzuAuditRetentionFailing` | The pass is **erroring**, not declining. | `yuzu_server_audit_cleanup_failed_total` in the metric table below. |
| `YuzuAuditRetentionNotRunning` | The reaper is not running on a database that has run before --- retention is unenforced and the audit store grows without bound. Fires at every restart cadence and stays firing while the reaper is dead (the #2854 rung D redesign closed the old grace's blind band; coverage is machine-checked against [`prometheus/blind-band-measurement.md`](../prometheus/blind-band-measurement.md)'s committed manifest on every PR). | Triage is in [`ops-runbooks/audit-store-clock-guard.md`](../ops-runbooks/audit-store-clock-guard.md#yuzuauditretentionnotrunning) --- **start there**: it opens with the routine, self-clearing causes (a Prometheus restart false-pages for ~45 minutes) before the real diagnosis. Then `yuzu_server_audit_retention_passes_total` in the metric table below. |
| `YuzuAuditRetentionNeverRan` | NO retention pass has EVER run against this database --- not "not recently": the seeded stamp still reads 0 after three hours. Almost always a store that failed to open at boot, or a fresh install crash-looping before its first pass. | [`ops-runbooks/audit-store-clock-guard.md`](../ops-runbooks/audit-store-clock-guard.md#yuzuauditretentionneverran). |
| `YuzuAuditRetentionMetricMissing` | The liveness metric is not being scraped **at all**, so `YuzuAuditRetentionNotRunning` cannot fire for anyone. **Check `up` for the Yuzu target first** — if it is down, or the target has left service discovery, this is an outage rather than just a monitoring gap, and on a single-server Prometheus this may be the only rule firing. | The three-state triage is in [`ops-runbooks/audit-store-clock-guard.md`](../ops-runbooks/audit-store-clock-guard.md#yuzuauditretentionmetricmissing) — start there; then `yuzu_server_audit_retention_passes_total` in the metric table below. |
| `YuzuAuditRetentionStateNotPersisting` | The durable clock reading cannot be written, so detection will not survive a restart. | `yuzu_server_audit_retention_persist_failed_total` in the metric table below. |
| `YuzuAuditRetentionCapBinding` | Expiry is outrunning the drain. | [Capacity](#capacity). |
| `YuzuAuditRetentionAnchorNotSurviving` | The durable clock reading keeps being lost, so the guard re-bootstraps instead of comparing. Nothing was deleted by those passes. | [`ops-runbooks/audit-store-clock-guard.md`](../ops-runbooks/audit-store-clock-guard.md#yuzuauditretentionanchornotsurviving). |

Retention is driven by the server's wall clock. A cleanup pass that looks like it
would destroy the audit trail declines instead of deleting: it logs a warning and
adds one to `yuzu_server_audit_clock_anomaly_skips_total`. **A decline deletes
nothing.** It is the protection working, not evidence lost. The rest of this
section is about declines.

**Retention sweeps fleet-wide from a single process.** On the PostgreSQL
substrate every replica runs the cleanup thread, but each pass first tries to
take an advisory lease (`pg_try_advisory_xact_lock('audit_store:reap')`); only
the lease winner sweeps, so exactly one process prunes per tick across the whole
fleet. The 25,000-rows-per-pass cap therefore stays calibrated as a single
drain rate — it can never become "N replicas × 25,000." The clock guard's dedup
state now lives durably in the `audit_retention_meta` table (the previous pass's
serialized fact-set, alongside the last clock reading), so a condition reported
by one replica is not re-reported by another, and the state survives a restart
or a failover to a different replica. (Previously, on the single SQLite server,
this dedup state was an in-process member — correct for one writer, but it does
not port to a multi-replica store.)

**Dedup-semantics change (multi-replica correctness).** Because the dedup state
is now shared across replicas, an identical-magnitude clock step that repeats
with the *same* fact-set is reported **once** and then stands down, rather than
re-incrementing `yuzu_server_audit_clock_anomaly_skips_total` on every pass as
the single-process guard did. A genuinely *different* anomaly (for example a
dead-CMOS boot followed by an NTP correction — a distinct fact-set) still
